<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 09 (tethers)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure and potential complications associated with spinal cord tethering, specifically related to congenital anomalies and their clinical presentation. (识别脊髓固定综合征相关的解剖结构和潜在并发症，特别是与先天性异常及其临床表现相关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric neurology clinic with a history of recurrent urinary tract infections (UTIs) since infancy, progressive lower extremity weakness, and gait disturbance described as "waddling." He was born full-term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery. Physical examination reveals a prominent tuft of hair overlying the lower lumbar spine (L5-S1 region), a dimple approximately 3 cm above the hair tuft, and decreased sensation to light touch along the medial aspect of both feet and lower legs. Motor examination shows 4/5 strength in bilateral hip flexion and knee extension, with 3/5 strength in ankle dorsiflexion and plantarflexion. Deep tendon reflexes are hyperactive at the knees (3+) and ankles (2+), with bilateral Babinski signs present. MRI of the lumbar spine reveals a low-lying conus medullaris terminating at the L3 vertebral level, with a thickened filum terminale.

Which of the following structures is most likely responsible for the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Arachnoid granulations
(B) Filum terminale
(C) Dorsal root ganglion
(D) Cauda equina
(E) Epidural fat pad

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age (3 years old):** Suggests a congenital or early-onset condition.
- **Recurrent UTIs:** Often associated with neurogenic bladder dysfunction, which can result from spinal cord abnormalities affecting sacral nerve roots.
- **Progressive lower extremity weakness & waddling gait:** Indicates motor deficits, likely affecting the lower limbs, potentially due to spinal cord compression or dysfunction.
- **Hair tuft & spinal dimple (L5-S1):** Classic cutaneous markers suggestive of an underlying spinal dysraphism, such as spina bifida occulta or a tethered cord.
- **Decreased sensation (medial feet/lower legs):** Points to sensory deficits, potentially involving specific dermatomes or nerve roots.
- **Hyperactive reflexes (knees 3+, ankles 2+) & Babinski signs:** Indicate upper motor neuron signs, suggesting involvement of the spinal cord itself above the level of the lesion.
- **MRI findings (low-lying conus at L3, thickened filum terminale):** Confirms the presence of a tethered cord, where the spinal cord is abnormally attached and restricted in its normal ascent during development, leading to traction and potential damage. The thickened filum terminale is the specific structure causing the tethering.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Development:** During fetal development, the spinal cord extends the entire length of the vertebral canal. As the vertebral column grows faster than the spinal cord, the conus medullaris normally ascends to its adult position around the L1-L2 vertebral level by birth.
- **Filum Terminale:** The filum terminale is a thin strand of pia mater that extends from the apex of the conus medullaris to the coccyx. It anchors the spinal cord loosely within the vertebral canal.
- **Tethered Cord Syndrome:** This occurs when the filum terminale or other structures abnormally attach the spinal cord to surrounding tissues (e.g., vertebral column, dura, skin), preventing its normal ascent. This causes traction on the conus medullaris and nerve roots as the child grows, leading to progressive neurological deficits.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Symptoms often present in infancy or early childhood and include lower extremity weakness, gait abnormalities, bowel/bladder dysfunction (neurogenic bladder/bowel), foot deformities, and sensory changes. The specific deficits depend on the level of the conus medullaris and the affected nerve roots. The upper motor neuron signs (hyperreflexia, Babinski) indicate cord involvement, while lower motor neuron signs (weakness, atrophy) can indicate nerve root involvement.
- **Anatomical Location:** The hair tuft and dimple are located over the lumbosacral region (L5-S1), consistent with the typical location of spinal dysraphisms. The MRI confirms the low-lying conus (L3) and the thickened filum terminale, which is the direct cause of the tethering.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Arachnoid granulations:** These are structures within the dural venous sinuses that allow cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) to be reabsorbed into the venous circulation. They are located primarily in the superior sagittal sinus and transverse sinuses. They are not involved in anchoring the spinal cord and are not associated with tethered cord syndrome.
- (C) **Dorsal root ganglion:** These ganglia contain the cell bodies of sensory neurons. They are located along the dorsal roots of spinal nerves. While dorsal root ganglia can be involved in certain spinal cord anomalies (e.g., neurofibromatosis), they are not the primary structure responsible for tethering the conus medullaris in this classic presentation.
- (D) **Cauda equina:** This refers to the bundle of spinal nerve roots (L2-S5) that descend within the vertebral canal below the level of the conus medullaris. While the cauda equina can be affected by tethered cord syndrome (nerve root compression), it is not the structure *causing* the tethering itself. The tethering is caused by an abnormal attachment higher up, restricting the movement of the conus and the filum terminale.
- (E) **Epidural fat pad:** This is a layer of adipose tissue located in the epidural space surrounding the spinal cord and nerve roots. It provides cushioning. While an abnormally large epidural fat pad can sometimes contribute to tethering, the primary structure identified on MRI as thickened and causing the tethering in this case is the filum terminale. The filum terminale is the most common cause of tethered cord syndrome.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Function                                                                 | Role in Tethered Cord | Clinical Relevance                                                                 |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Filum Terminale**  | Extends from conus medullaris to coccyx   | Anchors spinal cord loosely                                                 | **Primary cause**      | Thickening/abnormal attachment restricts cord ascent, causing traction & damage. |
| Arachnoid Granulations| Dural venous sinuses (superior sagittal) | CSF reabsorption                                                           | None                  | Not related to tethered cord.                                                      |
| Dorsal Root Ganglion | Along dorsal spinal nerve roots            | Sensory neuron cell bodies                                                 | Possible involvement   | Can be affected by other conditions, but not the primary tethering structure.       |
| Cauda Equina         | Below conus medullaris (L2-S5)             | Bundle of spinal nerve roots                                                | Affected by tethering | Can be compressed by tethering, but doesn't cause it.                             |
| Epidural Fat Pad     | Epidural space                             | Cushioning                                                                | Possible contributor  | Can contribute, but filum terminale is the most common cause.                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tethered cord syndrome is caused by an abnormal attachment of the spinal cord, most commonly the filum terminale, preventing its normal ascent during growth. This leads to progressive neurological deficits, often presenting in childhood. Key clinical features include lower extremity weakness, gait disturbance, bowel/bladder dysfunction, and cutaneous markers like hair tufts or dimples over the lumbosacral spine. MRI is crucial for diagnosis, showing a low-lying conus medullaris and often a thickened filum terminale. Differentiating tethered cord from other causes of progressive neurological deficits in children (e.g., spinal muscular atrophy, Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease, tumors) requires careful clinical evaluation and imaging.

<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 10 (spinal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between anterior spinal artery syndrome and Brown-Séquard syndrome, understanding the vascular supply and functional organization of the spinal cord. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分前脊动脉综合征和Brown-Séquard综合征，理解脊髓的血管供应和功能组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe lower back pain followed by bilateral leg weakness and numbness. He reports an inability to feel light touch or temperature changes below the level of his umbilicus (T10 dermatome). Motor examination reveals paraparesis with preserved strength in the upper extremities. Sensory testing shows loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 bilaterally, but preservation of proprioception and vibration sense. Bowel and bladder control are initially intact, but he develops urinary retention within 12 hours. An emergent MRI of the thoracic spine reveals an acute infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at the T10 level.

Which of the following best describes the primary anatomical structure responsible for the patient's specific sensory deficits?

(A) Dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus)
(B) Lateral corticospinal tracts
(C) Ventral (anterior) horns
(D) Lateral spinothalamic tracts
(E) Dorsal root ganglia

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Sudden onset lower back pain, bilateral leg weakness/numbness:** Suggests an acute spinal cord event.
- **Inability to feel light touch/temperature below T10:** Points to loss of pain and temperature sensation in a specific dermatomal distribution (T10 and below).
- **Preserved proprioception/vibration:** Indicates intact dorsal column function.
- **Paraparesis (bilateral leg weakness):** Suggests involvement of motor pathways.
- **Upper extremity strength preserved:** Localizes the lesion to the thoracic spinal cord (below C5/C6).
- **Urinary retention:** Suggests involvement of autonomic pathways, often associated with spinal cord lesions.
- **MRI showing infarction of anterior 2/3 of spinal cord at T10:** This is the crucial piece of information localizing the lesion and its extent.
- **Question asks for the structure responsible for *specific sensory deficits* (light touch/temperature loss):** This directs the focus to the sensory pathways affected by the infarction.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord is functionally divided into dorsal (posterior) and ventral (anterior) halves.
    - **Dorsal Horns:** Receive sensory information. The dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus) are located most dorsally and carry fine touch, proprioception, and vibration. The substantia gelatinosa is located more ventrally within the dorsal horn and is involved in pain and temperature modulation.
    - **Ventral Horns:** Contain motor neurons (alpha and gamma).
    - **Lateral Horns (Thoracic/Lumbar):** Contain preganglionic sympathetic neurons.
    - **White Matter Tracts:**
        - **Dorsal Columns:** Located posteriorly, carry fine touch, proprioception, vibration (medial lemniscus pathway).
        - **Lateral Corticospinal Tracts:** Located laterally, carry motor commands for voluntary movement (descending pathway).
        - **Lateral Spinothalamic Tracts:** Located laterally, carry pain and temperature sensation (ascending pathway).
        - **Anterior Spinothalamic Tracts:** Located anteriorly, carry crude touch and pressure sensation (ascending pathway).
        - **Anterior Corticospinal Tracts:** Located anteriorly, carry motor commands (descending pathway).
- **Vascular Supply:** The spinal cord receives blood supply primarily from the anterior spinal artery (ASA) and the paired posterior spinal arteries (PSA).
    - **ASA:** Supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, including the ventral horns, anterior spinothalamic tracts, anterior corticospinal tracts, and the central portion of the lateral spinothalamic tracts.
    - **PSA:** Supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord, including the dorsal columns, dorsal horn neurons, and the lateral portion of the lateral spinothalamic tracts.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The MRI finding of an infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at T10 directly implicates the vascular territory of the anterior spinal artery. This territory includes the lateral spinothalamic tracts (carrying pain and temperature), the anterior spinothalamic tracts (carrying crude touch/pressure), the ventral horns (motor neurons), and the corticospinal tracts (motor control).
- **Patient's Deficits:** The patient exhibits loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 (consistent with lateral spinothalamic tract involvement), preserved proprioception/vibration (consistent with dorsal column sparing), and paraparesis (consistent with corticospinal tract involvement). The specific sensory deficit mentioned in the question (light touch/temperature loss) is primarily mediated by the spinothalamic tracts, which are located within the anterior two-thirds of the cord.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The spinal cord develops from the neural tube, which forms from the ectoderm.
- The dorsal part of the neural tube gives rise to the dorsal horn and dorsal columns.
- The ventral part gives rise to the ventral horn, lateral horn (in thoracic/lumbar regions), and the spinothalamic and corticospinal tracts.
- The anterior spinal artery arises from branches of the vertebral arteries.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus):** These tracts carry fine touch, proprioception, and vibration. The patient *retains* these sensations, indicating the dorsal columns are spared. The dorsal columns are supplied by the posterior spinal arteries, which were not affected by the anterior infarction. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (B) **Lateral corticospinal tracts:** These tracts carry motor commands for voluntary movement. Damage would cause contralateral weakness below the lesion level (in the limbs) or ipsilateral weakness (in the trunk). While the patient has paraparesis (likely due to corticospinal tract involvement in the infarction), the question specifically asks about the *sensory* deficits (loss of light touch and temperature). Therefore, this option is incorrect as it addresses motor function, not the specific sensory loss queried.
- (C) **Ventral (anterior) horns:** These contain lower motor neurons. Damage causes flaccid paralysis, muscle atrophy, and fasciculations at the level of the lesion. While the infarction likely involves the ventral horns contributing to the paraparesis, this structure is primarily responsible for motor output, not the specific sensory deficits of pain and temperature loss. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E) **Dorsal root ganglia:** These contain the cell bodies of primary sensory neurons. They are located outside the spinal cord. While damage to the dorsal roots would cause sensory loss, the lesion described is an infarction *within* the spinal cord itself, affecting ascending tracts. The dorsal root ganglia are not the primary structure responsible for the sensory deficits *within* the cord pathway. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location within Spinal Cord | Function                                      | Vascular Supply (Primary) | Patient's Status (Based on Vignette) |
| :--------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------------------------- |
| Dorsal Columns               | Posterior                   | Fine touch, proprioception, vibration         | Posterior Spinal Arteries | Intact                               |
| Lateral Corticospinal Tracts | Lateral                     | Voluntary motor control (descending)          | Anterior Spinal Artery    | Affected (causing paraparesis)      |
| Ventral Horns                | Ventral                     | Lower motor neurons (motor output)            | Anterior Spinal Artery    | Affected (contributing to paraparesis) |
| Lateral Spinothalamic Tracts | Lateral                     | Pain, temperature (ascending)                 | Anterior Spinal Artery    | Affected (causing sensory loss)     |
| Anterior Spinothalamic Tracts| Anterior                    | Crude touch, pressure (ascending)             | Anterior Spinal Artery    | Affected (contributing to sensory loss)|
| Dorsal Root Ganglia          | Outside Spinal Cord         | Cell bodies of primary sensory neurons        | N/A                      | N/A (not the site of the lesion)     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior spinal artery syndrome results from occlusion of the ASA, typically affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. This leads to loss of motor function (corticospinal tracts), pain and temperature sensation (spinothalamic tracts), and autonomic function below the level of the lesion, while sparing proprioception and vibration (dorsal columns). Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection causing ipsilateral motor/proprioception loss and contralateral pain/temperature loss) and posterior spinal artery syndrome (dorsal column involvement causing loss of proprioception/vibration). The specific sensory deficit of pain and temperature loss is mediated by the spinothalamic tracts located in the lateral and anterior white matter columns, which are supplied by the ASA.

### Question 111: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 11 (bottom)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between lesions affecting the conus medullaris, cauda equina, and filum terminale, including associated embryological development and clinical syndromes. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，特别是区分锥体末端、马尾神经和丝状体损伤，包括相关的胚胎发生和临床综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe low back pain radiating down both legs. He reports progressive bilateral lower extremity weakness, numbness in the saddle area (perineum, buttocks, inner thighs), and urinary retention over the past 24 hours. Physical examination reveals bilateral lower extremity weakness (MRC grade 3/5), decreased sensation to light touch below the T10 dermatome level, absent ankle reflexes bilaterally, and fecal incontinence. An MRI of the lumbar spine shows a large, centrally located disc herniation at the L1-L2 level compressing the spinal canal. The herniation appears to be impinging on the distal end of the spinal cord and the nerve roots exiting below it.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by this compression, leading to the patient's constellation of symptoms?

(A) The dorsal root ganglia of the L1 and L2 spinal nerves.
(B) The conus medullaris.
(C) The filum terminale.
(D) The lumbar cistern.
(E) The cauda equina nerve roots.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with diabetes (risk factor for neuropathy, but acute onset points to mechanical compression).
- **Chief Complaint:** Acute severe low back pain radiating bilaterally, progressive bilateral lower extremity weakness, saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, fecal incontinence.
- **Physical Exam:** Bilateral LE weakness (3/5), sensory loss below T10, absent ankle reflexes, saddle anesthesia, fecal incontinence.
- **Imaging:** Large central disc herniation at L1-L2 compressing the spinal canal, impinging on the distal spinal cord and nerve roots.
- **Key Clues:** The combination of bilateral lower extremity weakness, sensory loss below a specific dermatome (T10), saddle anesthesia, bowel/bladder dysfunction (urinary retention, fecal incontinence), and absent reflexes points towards a lesion affecting the distal spinal cord and/or the nerve roots exiting below it. The location of the herniation (L1-L2) is crucial. The spinal cord typically ends around the L1-L2 vertebral level in adults.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Termination:** The adult spinal cord terminates as the conus medullaris, typically at the L1-L2 vertebral level. Below this level, the spinal nerves descend within the vertebral canal as the cauda equina ("horse's tail").
- **Conus Medullaris:** This is the tapered, conical end of the spinal cord. It contains the motor neurons for the lower limbs and sacral parasympathetic outflow (S2-S4), which controls bladder and bowel function. Damage here typically causes bilateral lower extremity weakness, sensory loss below the level of the lesion, and bowel/bladder dysfunction. Reflexes may be initially hyperactive (spinal shock) but become hypoactive or absent later.
- **Cauda Equina:** This consists of the lumbar, sacral, and coccygeal spinal nerve roots descending from the conus medullaris. Compression of the cauda equina typically causes radicular pain (sciatica), lower extremity weakness (often asymmetric), sensory loss in a dermatomal pattern, and bowel/bladder dysfunction. Reflexes are usually diminished or absent early.
- **Filum Terminale:** This is a thin strand of pia mater that extends from the tip of the conus medullaris to the coccyx, anchoring the spinal cord. It is generally not a primary site of pathology causing these symptoms, although inflammation (e.g., lipoma) can occur.
- **Lumbar Cistern:** This is a subarachnoid space in the lumbar region, containing cerebrospinal fluid (CSF). While a herniation can compress the CSF space, the primary pathology affecting neurological function is the compression of neural tissue (conus or cauda equina).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (bilateral weakness, sensory loss below T10, saddle anesthesia, bowel/bladder dysfunction) strongly suggest involvement of the conus medullaris. The L1-L2 herniation is precisely at the level where the spinal cord terminates. Compression at this level affects the distal spinal cord (conus) and potentially the immediately exiting nerve roots (part of the cauda equina). The bilateral nature of the weakness and the presence of saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction are classic signs of conus medullaris syndrome. The sensory level (T10) is slightly higher than the L1-L2 level, which can occur due to the ascending sensory tracts within the cord being affected before the lesion reaches the L1-L2 level itself, or due to referred pain/sensory changes. However, the core features point strongly to conus involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **The dorsal root ganglia of the L1 and L2 spinal nerves:** These contain the cell bodies of sensory neurons. While compression could affect them, it wouldn't typically cause bilateral motor weakness, saddle anesthesia (which involves motor and sensory components), or bowel/bladder dysfunction originating from the spinal cord itself. Sensory loss would be limited to the L1/L2 dermatomes. This option doesn't explain the full clinical picture.
- (C) **The filum terminale:** This is a fibrous extension of the pia mater. While abnormalities can occur (e.g., lipoma), direct compression causing this acute, severe neurological deficit is unlikely. The filum terminale itself doesn't contain significant neural tissue responsible for motor control, sensation, or bowel/bladder function in this manner.
- (D) **The lumbar cistern:** This is the CSF space. Compression of the cistern itself doesn't directly cause the specific neurological deficits described (motor weakness, sensory loss pattern, bowel/bladder dysfunction). The symptoms arise from compression of the neural elements (conus medullaris or cauda equina) within the canal.
- (E) **The cauda equina nerve roots:** Compression of the cauda equina typically causes radicular pain, often asymmetric weakness, sensory loss in dermatomal patterns, and bowel/bladder dysfunction. While the herniation does compress the cauda equina roots *below* the conus, the *bilateral* lower extremity weakness, *saddle anesthesia*, and *bowel/bladder dysfunction* are more characteristic of conus medullaris involvement, which is located *above* the cauda equina roots at the L1-L2 level. Cauda equina syndrome often presents with more prominent radicular pain and less symmetric motor/sensory loss compared to conus medullaris syndrome. Given the location of the herniation at L1-L2 and the specific constellation of symptoms (especially bilateral weakness and saddle anesthesia), conus medullaris compression is the most likely primary pathology.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Conus Medullaris (B)                                  | Cauda Equina (E)                                     | Filum Terminale (C)                               | Dorsal Root Ganglia (A)                             |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Tapered end of spinal cord (L1-L2)                     | Nerve roots below conus medullaris (L1-S5)            | Extension from conus to coccyx                     | Located within intervertebral foramina (sensory only) |
| **Contents**         | Motor neurons (LMNs), ascending/descending tracts, S2-S4 parasympathetic | Spinal nerve roots (motor & sensory)                 | Pia mater                                          | Sensory neuron cell bodies                          |
| **Typical Symptoms** | Bilateral LE weakness, saddle anesthesia, bowel/bladder dysfunction | Radicular pain, asymmetric LE weakness, dermatomal sensory loss, bowel/bladder dysfunction | Usually asymptomatic unless pathology (e.g., lipoma) | Sensory loss in specific dermatomes                 |
| **Reflexes**         | Initially hyperactive (spinal shock), then hypoactive/absent | Diminished/absent                                    | N/A                                                | N/A                                                  |
| **Embryology**       | Derived from neural tube                               | Derived from neural tube (nerve roots)                | Derived from pia mater                              | Derived from neural crest cells                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key distinction lies in recognizing the clinical syndromes associated with lesions at the termination of the spinal cord (conus medullaris) versus the nerve roots below it (cauda equina). Conus medullaris syndrome typically presents with bilateral lower extremity weakness, saddle anesthesia, and bowel/bladder dysfunction due to involvement of the motor neurons and parasympathetic outflow within the cord itself. Cauda equina syndrome often presents with more prominent radicular pain and asymmetric findings. The level of the lesion (L1-L2 in this case) is critical for localizing the pathology to the conus medullaris.

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 12 (vertebral)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between anterior spinal artery syndrome and Brown-Séquard syndrome, understanding the vascular supply and functional organization of the spinal cord. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分前脊动脉综合征和Brown-Séquard综合征，理解脊髓的血管供应和功能组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe lower back pain followed by bilateral leg weakness and sensory loss below the T10 level. Initial examination reveals complete paralysis of the legs (motor strength 0/5 bilaterally), loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 bilaterally, and preserved proprioception and vibration sense below T10 bilaterally. Bowel and bladder function are also impaired. An emergent MRI of the thoracic spine reveals an acute infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at the T10 level.

Which of the following best describes the likely underlying vascular event responsible for this patient's presentation?

(A) Occlusion of the posterior spinal arteries bilaterally.
(B) Occlusion of the anterior spinal artery.
(C) Hemisection of the spinal cord affecting the left side.
(D) Occlusion of the vertebral arteries bilaterally.
(E) Occlusion of the posterior median sulcus artery.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Sudden onset lower back pain followed by bilateral leg weakness and sensory loss below T10:** Suggests an acute spinal cord event at the T10 level.
- **Complete paralysis of the legs (motor strength 0/5 bilaterally):** Indicates involvement of the corticospinal tracts bilaterally.
- **Loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 bilaterally:** Indicates involvement of the spinothalamic tracts bilaterally.
- **Preserved proprioception and vibration sense below T10 bilaterally:** Indicates sparing of the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus) bilaterally.
- **Bowel and bladder function impaired:** Suggests involvement of autonomic pathways, often located in the lateral horns (thoracic/lumbar) or descending autonomic tracts.
- **MRI showing acute infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at T10:** This is the key imaging finding confirming the location and extent of the lesion.
- **Clinical syndrome:** The combination of bilateral motor weakness (corticospinal tracts), bilateral loss of pain/temperature (spinothalamic tracts), and preserved proprioception/vibration (dorsal columns) is characteristic of anterior spinal artery syndrome.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Vascular Supply:** The spinal cord receives blood supply primarily from the anterior spinal artery (ASA) and the paired posterior spinal arteries (PSAs).
    - **Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA):** Arises from the vertebral arteries, runs down the anterior median fissure, and supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. This includes the anterior horns (motor neurons), lateral corticospinal tracts (motor control), lateral spinothalamic tracts (pain/temperature), and the central autonomic pathways. It also supplies the anterior median sulcus artery, which runs along the anterior median fissure.
    - **Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSAs):** Arise from the vertebral arteries or the posterior inferior cerebellar arteries (PICAs), run along the posterolateral sulci, and supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord. This includes the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus - proprioception/vibration), the posterior horns (sensory neurons), and the posterior spinocerebellar tracts.
- **Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome:** Results from occlusion of the ASA. The anterior two-thirds of the cord is infarcted, leading to:
    - Bilateral loss of motor function below the lesion (corticospinal tracts).
    - Bilateral loss of pain and temperature sensation below the lesion (spinothalamic tracts).
    - Sparing of proprioception and vibration sense below the lesion (dorsal columns).
    - Bowel/bladder dysfunction (autonomic pathways).
- **Brown-Séquard Syndrome:** Results from hemisection of the spinal cord. It causes ipsilateral motor loss and loss of proprioception/vibration, and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation below the lesion.
- **Posterior Spinal Artery Syndrome:** Results from occlusion of the PSAs. It causes loss of proprioception and vibration sense bilaterally below the lesion, with sparing of motor function, pain, and temperature sensation.
- **Vertebral Artery Occlusion:** Can lead to various brainstem syndromes (e.g., Wallenberg syndrome) or spinal cord ischemia if it affects the ASA origin, but bilateral vertebral artery occlusion is less likely to cause isolated anterior spinal cord syndrome.
- **Posterior Median Sulcus Artery:** A branch of the ASA, occlusion would affect only a small portion of the anterior cord, not the entire anterior two-thirds.

The patient's clinical presentation (bilateral motor and pain/temperature loss below T10 with preserved proprioception/vibration) and MRI findings (infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the cord) are classic for anterior spinal artery syndrome. This syndrome is most commonly caused by occlusion of the anterior spinal artery itself.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Occlusion of the posterior spinal arteries bilaterally would affect the posterior one-third of the spinal cord, leading to bilateral loss of proprioception and vibration sensation below the lesion, while motor function, pain, and temperature sensation would be spared. This is the opposite of the patient's presentation.
- (C): Hemisection of the spinal cord causes Brown-Séquard syndrome, characterized by ipsilateral motor loss and loss of proprioception/vibration, and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation. The patient has bilateral motor and sensory loss (pain/temperature) with spared proprioception/vibration, which is inconsistent with hemisection.
- (D): Occlusion of the vertebral arteries bilaterally could potentially compromise the ASA supply, but it typically causes more widespread ischemia, often involving the brainstem. Isolated anterior spinal cord syndrome is less likely from bilateral vertebral artery occlusion compared to direct ASA occlusion. Furthermore, the clinical picture points specifically to the ASA territory.
- (E): The posterior median sulcus artery is a small branch of the ASA. Occlusion of this single branch would cause a very limited infarct, not affecting the entire anterior two-thirds of the cord as seen on the MRI.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome (ASA Occlusion) | Brown-Séquard Syndrome (Hemisection) | Posterior Spinal Artery Syndrome (PSA Occlusion) |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Lesion Location**     | Anterior 2/3 of cord                          | One side of cord                     | Posterior 1/3 of cord                          |
| **Motor Function**      | Bilateral loss below lesion                    | Ipsilateral loss below lesion         | Spared below lesion                             |
| **Pain/Temp Sensation** | Bilateral loss below lesion                    | Contralateral loss below lesion       | Spared below lesion                             |
| **Proprioception/Vibration**| Spared below lesion                             | Ipsilateral loss below lesion         | Bilateral loss below lesion                    |
| **Autonomic Function**  | Often impaired                                 | Variable                              | Usually spared                                  |
| **Vascular Supply**     | Anterior Spinal Artery                         | N/A                                  | Posterior Spinal Arteries                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior spinal artery syndrome results from ischemia of the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, typically due to ASA occlusion. It causes bilateral loss of motor function and pain/temperature sensation below the lesion, with preservation of proprioception and vibration. Differentiating this from Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and posterior spinal artery syndrome (PSA occlusion) is crucial based on the pattern of sensory and motor deficits. Remember the mnemonic "ASA affects anterior 2/3, sparing the posterior 1/3". Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and posterior spinal artery syndrome.

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 13 (column)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of a spinal cord lesion based on specific sensory and motor deficits, understanding the dermatomal and myotomal innervation patterns associated with specific spinal cord segments, specifically related to the C8-T1 region and its clinical manifestations. (根据特定的感觉和运动功能障碍定位脊髓损伤，理解与特定脊髓节段相关的皮节和肌节支配模式，特别是与C8-T1区域及其临床表现相关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after falling 15 feet from scaffolding, landing on his back. He complains of severe lower back pain and bilateral leg weakness. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological assessment reveals intact sensation in the upper extremities and trunk. However, he has complete loss of sensation in the medial aspect of the left foot and the lateral aspect of the right foot. Motor examination shows weakness in dorsiflexion of the left foot (medical examiner notes 3/5 strength) and weakness in foot eversion of the right foot (4/5 strength). He is unable to perform heel walking on the left and toe walking on the right. Reflexes are absent in the Achilles tendons bilaterally. An MRI of the thoracolumbar spine reveals a severe compression fracture of the T1 vertebral body with significant retropulsion of bone fragments into the spinal canal.

Which spinal cord segment is most likely affected by this injury, causing the patient's specific pattern of sensory and motor deficits?

(A) C5-C6
(B) C7-C8
(C) T2-T3
(D) T1
(E) L4-L5

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Fall onto the back causing severe lower back pain suggests a spinal injury, specifically a vertebral fracture.
- **MRI Findings:** Compression fracture of T1 with retropulsion confirms a lesion at the T1 spinal cord level.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Loss of sensation on the medial left foot and lateral right foot points to a specific dermatomal pattern. The medial foot is typically innervated by the L4 dermatome (via the saphenous nerve), and the lateral foot is typically innervated by the S1 dermatome (via the sural nerve). However, the question asks for the *spinal cord segment* affected, not the dermatome itself. The injury is at T1, which affects the descending sensory tracts. The specific pattern of sensory loss (medial foot on one side, lateral foot on the other) is unusual and might suggest a partial lesion or specific nerve root involvement *secondary* to the spinal cord injury at T1, or potentially a misinterpretation of the sensory exam. However, the primary injury is clearly at T1. The question asks which spinal cord segment is *most likely affected*. Given the T1 fracture and retropulsion, the T1 spinal cord segment is the primary site of injury.
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in left foot dorsiflexion (heel walking impaired) involves the tibialis anterior muscle, primarily innervated by the deep peroneal nerve (L4, L5, S1 roots). Weakness in right foot eversion (toe walking impaired) involves the peroneus longus and brevis muscles, primarily innervated by the superficial peroneal nerve (L5, S1 roots). Again, these deficits point to L4, L5, and S1 nerve root involvement.
- **Reflexes:** Absent Achilles reflexes (S1) are consistent with lower motor neuron signs at the S1 level, potentially due to nerve root compression or spinal cord damage affecting the reflex arc.
- **Synthesis:** The primary injury is at the T1 vertebral level, compressing the T1 spinal cord segment. While the sensory and motor deficits described (L4, L5, S1 dermatomes/myotomes) seem more consistent with a lower lesion (L4-S1), the MRI findings definitively localize the *spinal cord injury* to T1. The question asks which spinal cord segment is affected. The T1 fracture directly impacts the T1 spinal cord segment. The observed deficits likely represent a combination of direct T1 cord damage and potential secondary compression or damage to the conus medullaris or cauda equina roots (L4-S1) due to the retropulsion of bone fragments. However, the *primary* spinal cord segment affected by the T1 fracture is T1 itself.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Segmentation:** The spinal cord is divided into cervical (C1-C8), thoracic (T1-T12), lumbar (L1-L5), sacral (S1-S5), and coccygeal segments. Each segment gives rise to paired spinal nerves.
- **T1 Spinal Cord Segment:** The T1 spinal cord segment is located at the level of the first thoracic vertebra. It receives sensory input from the T1 dermatome (skin over the upper back) and motor output to muscles innervated by the T1 spinal nerve (intrinsic back muscles).
- **Clinical Manifestations of T1 Lesion:** A lesion at the T1 spinal cord segment typically causes:
    - Sensory loss in the T1 dermatome.
    - Motor weakness in muscles innervated by T1 nerve roots (e.g., paraspinal muscles).
    - Potential involvement of descending tracts affecting lower limbs, depending on the extent of the lesion.
- **Dermatomes and Myotomes:**
    - L4: Medial leg and foot sensation, knee extension (quadriceps), ankle dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior).
    - L5: Lateral leg and dorsum of foot sensation, great toe extension (extensor hallucis longus), foot eversion (peroneus longus/brevis), ankle dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior).
    - S1: Lateral foot and sole sensation, ankle plantarflexion (gastrocnemius/soleus), foot eversion (peroneus longus/brevis), hip extension (gluteus maximus).
- **The Discrepancy:** The clinical presentation (L4-S1 deficits) doesn't perfectly match a pure T1 lesion. However, the MRI clearly shows a T1 vertebral fracture with retropulsion compressing the spinal cord at the T1 level. This compression likely affects the T1 segment directly and may also impinge on the conus medullaris (L1-L2) or cauda equina roots (L2-S5) below, explaining the L4-S1 deficits. The question asks for the *spinal cord segment* affected by the *injury* (T1 fracture). Therefore, T1 is the most direct answer based on the imaging findings. The sensory and motor findings are secondary consequences of the primary T1 cord injury and potential secondary compression.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) C5-C6: This segment is in the neck. A lesion here would cause deficits in the shoulder (deltoid, biceps - C5, C6) and upper arm (e.g., wrist extension - C6), and sensory loss over the lateral upper arm (C5) and thumb/index finger (C6). This is inconsistent with the lower back injury and lower limb deficits.
- (B) C7-C8: This segment is also in the neck. A lesion here would cause deficits in the forearm and hand (e.g., triceps - C7, finger extension - C7, finger flexion - C8), and sensory loss over the posterior arm/forearm (C7) and medial hand/ring/little finger (C8). This is inconsistent with the lower back injury and lower limb deficits.
- (C) T2-T3: This segment is slightly below T1. A lesion here would primarily affect the trunk muscles innervated by T2/T3 and potentially descending tracts. While it's in the thoracic region, the MRI specifically points to T1. A T2-T3 lesion wouldn't directly explain the T1 vertebral fracture. The sensory and motor deficits described are also more consistent with a lower lesion (L4-S1), although the primary injury site is T1.
- (E) L4-L5: This segment is in the lumbar region. A lesion here would cause deficits in the lower leg and foot (L4: knee extension, ankle dorsiflexion; L5: great toe extension, foot eversion, ankle dorsiflexion). While the motor deficits described (foot dorsiflexion, foot eversion) align with L4/L5 myotomes, the primary injury is clearly identified as a T1 vertebral fracture compressing the T1 spinal cord segment on MRI. A lesion *at* L4-L5 would not be caused by a T1 vertebral fracture.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature          | T1 Spinal Cord Segment | C5-C6 Spinal Cord Segment | C7-C8 Spinal Cord Segment | T2-T3 Spinal Cord Segment | L4-L5 Spinal Cord Segment |
|-------------------|------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Vertebral Level** | T1                     | C5-C6                    | C7-C8                    | T2-T3                    | L4-L5                    |
| **Primary Injury Site** | T1 Vertebra (MRI)     | Neck                     | Neck                     | Thoracic (T2-T3)         | Lumbar (L4-L5)           |
| **Dermatome**     | Upper Back             | Lateral Upper Arm, Thumb/Index Finger | Posterior Arm/Forearm, Medial Hand | Thoracic Trunk          | Medial Leg/Foot (L4), Lateral Leg/Dorsum Foot (L5) |
| **Myotome**       | Paraspinal Muscles     | Deltoid, Biceps          | Triceps, Finger Extensors | Trunk Muscles            | Knee Extensors (L4), Great Toe Extensors, Foot Evertors (L5) |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Upper back pain/sensory loss, potential lower limb deficits due to tract involvement/secondary compression | Shoulder/Upper Arm Weakness, Sensory Loss | Forearm/Hand Weakness, Sensory Loss | Trunk Weakness, Sensory Loss | Lower Leg/Foot Weakness, Sensory Loss |
| **Relevance to Vignette** | **Directly affected by T1 fracture** | Incorrect region         | Incorrect region         | Incorrect region (T1 fracture) | Incorrect region (T1 fracture), but matches some motor deficits |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is to correlate imaging findings (MRI showing T1 fracture) with the specific spinal cord segment affected. While the clinical presentation might suggest involvement of lower segments (L4-S1) due to descending tract damage or secondary compression of the conus/cauda equina, the primary injury site dictated by the vertebral fracture is the T1 spinal cord segment. Always prioritize the anatomical level indicated by the primary pathology (fracture/lesion). Differential diagnosis includes spinal cord contusion, hematoma, or edema at T1, potentially extending to involve descending motor and sensory tracts supplying the lower limbs, or compression of the conus medullaris/cauda equina.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 14 (bodies)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between anterior spinal artery syndrome and Brown-Séquard syndrome, understanding the vascular supply and functional organization of the spinal cord. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分前脊动脉综合征和Brown-Séquard综合征，理解脊髓的血管供应和功能组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of bilateral lower extremity weakness and sensory loss below the T10 level. He reports feeling a sharp pain in his back immediately prior to the onset of symptoms. On examination, his blood pressure is 160/95 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals complete motor paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation bilaterally below T10. However, proprioception and vibratory sensation are preserved below T10. Upper extremities show no motor or sensory deficits. An MRI of the thoracic spine reveals an acute infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at the T10 level.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the infarction, leading to this specific clinical presentation?

(A) Dorsal columns (Fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus)
(B) Lateral corticospinal tract
(C) Lateral spinothalamic tract
(D) Anterior spinal artery
(E) Posterior spinal arteries

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases suspicion for vascular events like stroke or spinal cord infarction.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset suggests an acute vascular event (infarction, hemorrhage) rather than a compressive lesion (tumor, disc herniation) which typically develops more gradually.
- **Location:** Symptoms below T10 level indicate a lesion at or above the T10 spinal cord segment.
- **Motor Deficits:** Bilateral lower extremity weakness/paralysis points to involvement of descending motor pathways (corticospinal tracts).
- **Sensory Deficits:**
    - Loss of pain and temperature sensation bilaterally below T10 indicates involvement of the spinothalamic tracts.
    - Preserved proprioception and vibration sensation below T10 indicates sparing of the dorsal columns.
- **Imaging:** MRI showing acute infarction affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at T10 confirms a vascular event localized to that region.
- **Clinical Syndrome:** The combination of bilateral motor weakness, loss of pain/temperature sensation, and preserved proprioception/vibration below a specific spinal level is characteristic of Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA) Syndrome.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Vascular Supply:** The spinal cord receives blood supply primarily from the anterior spinal artery (ASA) and the paired posterior spinal arteries (PSAs).
    - **ASA:** Arises from the vertebral arteries, runs down the anterior median fissure, and supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. This includes the anterior horns (motor neurons), lateral horns (sympathetic neurons), anterior and lateral corticospinal tracts, and the spinothalamic tracts.
    - **PSAs:** Arise from the vertebral arteries, run along the posterolateral sulci, and supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord. This includes the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus, responsible for proprioception, vibration, and fine touch) and the dorsal root ganglia.
- **Functional Organization:**
    - **Anterior 2/3:** Motor function (corticospinal tracts), pain/temperature sensation (spinothalamic tracts), autonomic function (lateral horns).
    - **Posterior 1/3:** Proprioception, vibration, fine touch (dorsal columns).
- **ASA Syndrome:** Occlusion of the ASA leads to infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. Clinically, this manifests as:
    - Bilateral motor weakness/paralysis below the level of the lesion (due to corticospinal tract involvement).
    - Bilateral loss of pain and temperature sensation below the level of the lesion (due to spinothalamic tract involvement).
    - Sparing of proprioception and vibration sensation (due to dorsal column sparing, as they are supplied by the PSAs).
    - Bowel/bladder dysfunction may occur due to involvement of autonomic pathways.
- **Patient's Presentation:** The patient's symptoms (bilateral motor loss, bilateral loss of pain/temperature, preserved proprioception/vibration below T10) perfectly match the clinical picture of ASA syndrome resulting from an infarction at T10. The MRI confirms infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the cord. Therefore, the affected structure is the anterior spinal artery itself, whose occlusion caused the infarction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Dorsal columns (Fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus):** These tracts carry proprioception, vibration, and fine touch. They are located in the posterior one-third of the spinal cord and are supplied by the posterior spinal arteries. Damage would cause loss of these sensations, which is *not* the primary finding in this patient (these sensations are preserved).
- (B) **Lateral corticospinal tract:** This tract carries motor commands. It is located in the lateral white matter, within the anterior two-thirds of the cord, and is supplied by the ASA. Damage would cause motor weakness/paralysis. While involved in this patient's syndrome, the question asks for the *structure* affected by the infarction causing the *entire* syndrome. The infarction affects the *artery* supplying multiple tracts, including the corticospinal tract, but also the spinothalamic tract. Therefore, the artery is the primary affected structure leading to the overall clinical picture.
- (C) **Lateral spinothalamic tract:** This tract carries pain and temperature sensation. It is located in the lateral white matter, within the anterior two-thirds of the cord, and is supplied by the ASA. Damage would cause loss of pain and temperature sensation. While involved in this patient's syndrome, similar to the corticospinal tract, it's only part of the affected territory. The infarction affects the *artery* supplying multiple tracts, including the spinothalamic tract, but also the corticospinal tract. Therefore, the artery is the primary affected structure leading to the overall clinical picture.
- (E) **Posterior spinal arteries:** These arteries supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord, including the dorsal columns. Occlusion would cause loss of proprioception and vibration, sparing motor and pain/temperature sensation, which is the opposite of this patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location in Spinal Cord | Blood Supply          | Function Affected                                      | Clinical Manifestation of Damage                                  |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------- | :-------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Anterior Spinal Artery** | Anterior 2/3            | Vertebral Arteries    | Motor (CST), Pain/Temp (STT), Autonomic               | Bilateral motor weakness, bilateral loss of pain/temp, spared proprioception/vibration (ASA Syndrome) |
| Dorsal Columns            | Posterior 1/3            | Posterior Spinal Arteries | Proprioception, Vibration, Fine Touch                 | Loss of proprioception, vibration, fine touch                     |
| Lateral Corticospinal Tract | Lateral White Matter    | Anterior Spinal Artery | Motor Control                                         | Ipsilateral motor weakness/paralysis (if lesion is unilateral) or Bilateral (if lesion is bilateral or ASA occlusion) |
| Lateral Spinothalamic Tract | Lateral White Matter    | Anterior Spinal Artery | Pain and Temperature Sensation                        | Ipsilateral loss of pain/temp sensation (if lesion is unilateral) or Bilateral (if lesion is bilateral or ASA occlusion) |
| Posterior Spinal Arteries  | Posterior 1/3            | Vertebral Arteries    | Proprioception, Vibration, Fine Touch                 | Loss of proprioception, vibration, fine touch                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA) syndrome results from occlusion of the ASA, causing infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. This leads to bilateral motor weakness and loss of pain/temperature sensation below the level of the lesion, with sparing of proprioception and vibration (carried by the dorsal columns supplied by the posterior spinal arteries). This contrasts with Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection), which causes ipsilateral motor loss and loss of proprioception/vibration, and contralateral loss of pain/temperature. Key differentials include compressive myelopathy (slower onset, often with upper motor neuron signs above the lesion), transverse myelitis (inflammatory), and spinal cord tumors.

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 15 (primary)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between anterior spinal artery syndrome and Brown-Séquard syndrome, understanding the vascular supply and functional organization of the spinal cord. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分前脊动脉综合征和Brown-Séquard综合征，理解脊髓的血管供应和功能组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of bilateral lower extremity weakness and sensory loss below the T10 level. He reports feeling cold in his feet. On examination, motor strength is 2/5 bilaterally in the lower extremities, with spasticity noted. Sensory examination reveals complete loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10, but preservation of light touch, vibration, and proprioception. Bowel and bladder function are intact. MRI of the thoracic spine reveals an infarct affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at the T10 level.

Which of the following vascular structures is most likely compromised in this patient?

(A) Posterior spinal arteries
(B) Anterior spinal artery
(C) Vertebral arteries
(D) Radicular arteries
(E) Vas anterior interosseous

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This suggests a vascular etiology for the neurological deficit.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset points towards an acute vascular event like an infarct.
- **Level:** Bilateral lower extremity weakness and sensory loss below T10 indicates a lesion at the T10 spinal cord level.
- **Motor Deficits:** Bilateral lower extremity weakness (2/5 strength) and spasticity suggest damage to the corticospinal tracts.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Complete loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10, with preservation of light touch, vibration, and proprioception. This pattern is crucial. Pain and temperature fibers enter the spinal cord via the dorsal root and ascend in the spinothalamic tract, located in the anterolateral white matter. Light touch, vibration, and proprioception travel in the dorsal columns, located in the posterior white matter. The preservation of dorsal column function while losing spinothalamic function points to damage in the anterior/anterolateral portion of the cord.
- **Autonomic Function:** Intact bowel and bladder function suggests the lesion is below the sacral parasympathetic outflow (S2-S4) and doesn't involve the autonomic centers extensively, or if it does, the damage is incomplete.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms an infarct affecting the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at T10. This directly correlates with the clinical findings.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the compromised vascular structure responsible for supplying the affected area.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Blood Supply:** The spinal cord receives blood supply from multiple sources.
    - **Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA):** Arises primarily from the vertebral arteries (though sometimes from the basilar artery or internal thoracic artery). It runs down the anterior median fissure of the spinal cord. It supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, including the anterior horns (motor neurons), the lateral corticospinal tracts (motor control), and the lateral spinothalamic tracts (pain and temperature sensation).
    - **Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSAs):** Arise from the vertebral arteries or the posterior inferior cerebellar arteries (PICAs). They run along the posterolateral sulci of the spinal cord. They supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord, including the posterior horns (sensory neurons), the dorsal columns (proprioception, vibration, fine touch), and the spinocerebellar tracts.
    - **Radicular Arteries:** Segmental branches arising directly from the vertebral, ascending cervical, intercostal, or lumbar arteries. They penetrate the spinal cord laterally and contribute to the ASA and PSAs, forming anastomotic networks.
- **Clinical Syndrome:** The patient presents with bilateral lower extremity weakness (corticospinal tract damage), loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 (spinothalamic tract damage), and preserved light touch, vibration, and proprioception (dorsal column preservation). This clinical picture, combined with the MRI finding of an infarct in the anterior two-thirds of the cord, is characteristic of **Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome**.
- **Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome:** Results from occlusion of the ASA or its major penetrating branches. It typically spares the dorsal columns (supplied by PSAs) and often spares some motor function if the lesion is incomplete or involves only the periphery of the anterior horns. The patient's symptoms perfectly match this syndrome.
- **Brown-Séquard Syndrome:** Results from hemisection of the spinal cord. It causes ipsilateral motor weakness and loss of proprioception/vibration/fine touch (dorsal column damage) and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation (spinothalamic tract damage crossing over). This does not match the patient's bilateral symptoms and sensory pattern.
- **Vertebral Arteries:** Supply the brainstem and cerebellum primarily, although they give rise to the ASA and PSAs. Occlusion of the vertebral arteries themselves would cause brainstem/cerebellar ischemia, not isolated spinal cord infarction.
- **Radicular Arteries:** While they contribute to the spinal cord circulation, occlusion of a single radicular artery is less likely to cause such a widespread infarct affecting the anterior two-thirds of the cord bilaterally.
- **Vas anterior interosseous:** This is a branch of the ulnar artery in the forearm, supplying muscles in the anterior compartment of the forearm. It is irrelevant to spinal cord blood supply.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations:**
- The spinal cord develops from the neural tube. The anterior two-thirds contain primarily motor neurons (anterior horn cells) and ascending/descending tracts related to motor control and pain/temperature sensation. The posterior one-third contains sensory neurons (posterior horn cells) and ascending tracts related to proprioception, vibration, and fine touch. This functional segregation correlates with the vascular supply.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Posterior spinal arteries:** These supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord (dorsal columns, posterior horns). Occlusion would cause loss of proprioception, vibration, and fine touch, with preservation of motor function and pain/temperature sensation. This is the opposite of the patient's presentation. Test-takers might confuse this because the posterior aspect of the cord is involved in sensory pathways, but the specific sensory modalities preserved vs. lost are key.
- (C) **Vertebral arteries:** These are major arteries supplying the brainstem and cerebellum, and they give rise to the ASA and PSAs. While crucial for spinal cord supply indirectly, occlusion of the vertebral artery itself typically causes brainstem or cerebellar symptoms (e.g., Wallenberg syndrome if PICA is affected), not isolated anterior spinal cord syndrome. Test-takers might choose this because they are the origin of the ASA, but the lesion is specifically within the spinal cord itself.
- (D) **Radicular arteries:** These are segmental arteries that contribute to the ASA and PSAs. While they are part of the spinal cord circulation, occlusion of a single radicular artery is unlikely to cause a large infarct affecting the entire anterior two-thirds of the cord bilaterally. They are more likely to cause smaller, localized lesions. Test-takers might choose this if they are thinking broadly about spinal cord blood supply but don't understand the primary vessel responsible for the anterior two-thirds.
- (E) **Vas anterior interosseous:** This artery is located in the forearm and supplies muscles there. It has no relation to the spinal cord. This is a pure distractor testing basic anatomical knowledge.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA) | Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSA) | Vertebral Arteries | Radicular Arteries |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------|--------------------|--------------------|
| **Location**         | Anterior median fissure     | Posterolateral sulci          | Outside spinal cord| Penetrate cord laterally |
| **Supply Area**      | Anterior 2/3 of cord        | Posterior 1/3 of cord         | Brainstem/Cerebellum, origin of ASA/PSA | Contribute to ASA/PSA |
| **Tracts Supplied**  | Corticospinal, Spinothalamic | Dorsal Columns, Spinocerebellar | N/A                | N/A                |
| **Clinical Deficit (Occlusion)** | Bilateral motor weakness, loss of pain/temp, preserved proprioception/vibration | Loss of proprioception/vibration/fine touch, preserved motor/pain/temp | Brainstem/Cerebellar signs | Variable, often smaller lesions |
| **Patient Presentation** | Matches ASA syndrome        | Opposite of patient          | Opposite of patient | Unlikely to cause this pattern |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome results from occlusion of the ASA, causing infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. This leads to bilateral loss of motor function (corticospinal tracts) and pain/temperature sensation (spinothalamic tracts) below the level of the lesion, with preservation of proprioception, vibration, and fine touch (dorsal columns). Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and Posterior Spinal Artery Syndrome. Always correlate clinical findings with the known vascular territories and functional organization of the spinal cord.

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 16 (sensory)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of a spinal cord lesion based on sensory deficits, specifically differentiating between dorsal column (proprioception, vibration, fine touch) and spinothalamic tract (pain, temperature, crude touch) pathways, and understanding the anatomical organization of the spinal cord white matter. (根据感觉缺失定位脊髓损伤，区分后索通路（本体感觉、振动、精细触觉）和脊髓丘脑束（痛觉、温度觉、粗触觉），理解脊髓白质的解剖组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall down a flight of stairs. He complains of severe back pain and bilateral lower extremity weakness. On examination, he exhibits paraplegia below the level of T10. Sensory testing reveals complete loss of pain and temperature sensation bilaterally below T10. However, proprioception, vibration sense, and fine touch are preserved bilaterally below T10. Motor examination confirms flaccid paralysis of all muscles below T10, with absent reflexes. An MRI of the thoracic spine shows a complete transection of the spinal cord at the T10 level. Which spinal cord tract is most likely responsible for the preserved proprioception, vibration sense, and fine touch sensation below the level of the lesion?

(A) Lateral spinothalamic tract
(B) Anterior spinothalamic tract
(C) Dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus)
(D) Ventral corticospinal tract
(E) Lateral corticospinal tract

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, fall, back pain, bilateral lower extremity weakness (paraplegia). Suggests spinal cord injury.
- **Neurological Examination:**
    - Paraplegia below T10: Indicates motor pathway involvement (corticospinal tracts).
    - Complete loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10: Indicates involvement of the spinothalamic tracts (both lateral and anterior).
    - Preserved proprioception, vibration sense, and fine touch below T10: This is the key finding. These sensations are mediated by the dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway.
- **MRI Findings:** Complete transection of the spinal cord at T10. This confirms a complete lesion at this level.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which tract is responsible for the *preserved* sensory modalities (proprioception, vibration, fine touch).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord white matter contains ascending (sensory) and descending (motor) tracts.
- **Sensory Pathways:**
    - **Dorsal Column-Medial Lemniscus Pathway:** Carries proprioception, vibration, fine touch, and two-point discrimination. The fibers enter the spinal cord via the dorsal roots, ascend ipsilaterally in the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis for lower body, fasciculus cuneatus for upper body), synapse in the nucleus gracilis and nucleus cuneatus in the medulla, decussate (cross over) in the medulla as the internal arcuate fibers, and ascend as the medial lemniscus to the thalamus (VPL nucleus).
    - **Spinothalamic Tract:** Carries pain, temperature, and crude touch. Fibers enter the spinal cord via the dorsal roots, synapse in the dorsal horn (substantia gelatinosa), and the secondary neurons decussate within 1-2 segments of entry (lateral spinothalamic tract) or at the level of entry (anterior spinothalamic tract). They then ascend contralaterally in the anterolateral white matter to the thalamus (VPL nucleus).
- **Motor Pathways:**
    - **Corticospinal Tract:** Carries motor commands from the cortex. The lateral corticospinal tract (originating from the motor cortex) decussates in the medulla (pyramids) and descends contralaterally. The anterior corticospinal tract (originating from the motor cortex and brainstem) descends ipsilaterally and decussates at the spinal level.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient has a complete spinal cord transection at T10.
    - Loss of pain/temperature below T10 indicates damage to the spinothalamic tracts (both lateral and anterior).
    - Loss of motor function below T10 indicates damage to the corticospinal tracts (both lateral and anterior).
    - Preservation of proprioception, vibration, and fine touch below T10 indicates that the dorsal columns are *spared* or only partially affected, despite the complete transection. This is unusual for a complete transection, but the question asks which tract *mediates* these preserved sensations. The dorsal columns are the anatomical structures responsible for carrying these specific sensory modalities. The question is testing knowledge of which pathway carries these specific sensations, not necessarily the exact pathophysiology of why they are preserved in this specific injury (which might imply an incomplete lesion despite the MRI description, or perhaps a very specific type of transection). However, based on the preserved sensory findings, the dorsal columns must be functionally intact for these modalities.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lateral spinothalamic tract:** Carries pain and temperature sensation. This tract is damaged in the patient, explaining the loss of these sensations below T10. Therefore, it is incorrect.
- (B) **Anterior spinothalamic tract:** Carries crude touch and pressure sensation. This tract is also damaged in the patient, contributing to the loss of sensation below T10. Therefore, it is incorrect.
- (D) **Ventral corticospinal tract:** This is a motor pathway (part of the corticospinal tract) responsible for motor control. Damage to this tract contributes to the paralysis below T10. It is a motor pathway, not a sensory pathway for proprioception/vibration/fine touch. Therefore, it is incorrect.
- (E) **Lateral corticospinal tract:** This is the primary motor pathway responsible for voluntary movement. Damage to this tract contributes significantly to the paralysis below T10. It is a motor pathway, not a sensory pathway for proprioception/vibration/fine touch. Therefore, it is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Dorsal Columns (Fasciculus Gracilis/Cuneatus) | Lateral Spinothalamic Tract | Anterior Spinothalamic Tract | Lateral Corticospinal Tract | Ventral Corticospinal Tract |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Modality Carried**    | Proprioception, Vibration, Fine Touch          | Pain, Temperature           | Crude Touch, Pressure        | Motor Control               | Motor Control               |
| **Location in Cord**    | Posterior White Matter                        | Anterolateral White Matter  | Anterolateral White Matter   | Lateral White Matter        | Ventral White Matter         |
| **Decussation**         | Medulla (Internal Arcuate Fibers)             | Spinal Cord (1-2 segments) | Spinal Cord (Level of entry) | Medulla (Pyramids)          | Spinal Cord (Level of entry) |
| **Effect of T10 Lesion** | Preserved below T10 (in this case)           | Lost below T10              | Lost below T10               | Lost below T10              | Lost below T10               |
| **Embryological Origin**| Alar Plate                                   | Basal Plate                 | Basal Plate                  | Basal Plate                 | Basal Plate                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway is responsible for carrying proprioception, vibration, and fine touch sensations. Lesions affecting this pathway result in loss of these modalities, while pain and temperature sensation (carried by the spinothalamic tracts) remain intact. Conversely, lesions affecting the spinothalamic tracts spare proprioception, vibration, and fine touch. This distinction is crucial for localizing spinal cord lesions. Key differentials include distinguishing between dorsal column lesions (e.g., tabes dorsalis, vitamin B12 deficiency) and spinothalamic tract lesions (e.g., Brown-Séquard syndrome, syringomyelia affecting the crossing fibers).
