<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 41 (funiculus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between dorsal column (proprioception, vibration, fine touch), lateral corticospinal tract (motor), and spinothalamic tract (pain, temperature) involvement, and understanding the anatomical organization within the spinal cord white matter tracts (funiculi). (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分背索（本体感觉、振动、精细触觉）、侧皮质脊髓束（运动）和脊髓丘脑束（痛觉、温度觉）的受累情况，以及理解脊髓白质束的解剖组织结构（脊髓索）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall down a flight of stairs. He complains of severe neck pain and bilateral upper extremity weakness. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological assessment reveals complete paralysis of all voluntary muscles below the level of T4. Sensory examination shows a complete loss of pain and temperature sensation below T4 bilaterally. However, proprioception and vibration sense are preserved below T4 bilaterally. Fine touch sensation is also intact below T4 bilaterally. Motor examination confirms flaccid paralysis of the lower extremities and trunk below T4, with absent deep tendon reflexes. An MRI of the cervical spine reveals a complete transverse fracture of the C4 vertebral body with significant retropulsion of bone fragments into the spinal canal, causing severe compression of the spinal cord at the C4-C5 level.

Which spinal cord tract is MOST likely spared, accounting for the preservation of proprioception and fine touch sensation below the level of the lesion?

(A) Lateral corticospinal tract
(B) Anterior corticospinal tract
(C) Fasciculus gracilis
(D) Fasciculus cuneatus
(E) Spinothalamic tract

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62M, fall, neck pain, bilateral upper extremity weakness. Suggests spinal cord injury.
- **Neurological Exam:**
    - Complete paralysis below T4: Indicates damage to descending motor tracts (corticospinal tracts).
    - Loss of pain/temperature below T4: Indicates damage to ascending sensory tracts (spinothalamic tracts).
    - Preservation of proprioception/vibration below T4: Indicates sparing of the dorsal columns.
    - Preservation of fine touch below T4: Indicates sparing of the dorsal columns.
    - Flaccid paralysis, absent reflexes below T4: Consistent with spinal shock following acute spinal cord injury.
- **MRI Findings:** C4 fracture with retropulsion compressing the spinal cord at C4-C5. This confirms the level of injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which tract is spared, explaining the preserved proprioception and fine touch.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord white matter is organized into dorsal (posterior), lateral, and ventral (anterior) columns (funiculi).
    - **Dorsal Columns (Posterior Funiculus):** Contains the fasciculus gracilis (medial, carries proprioception/vibration from lower limbs/trunk) and fasciculus cuneatus (lateral, carries proprioception/vibration from upper limbs/trunk). Also carries fine touch. These tracts ascend ipsilaterally and decussate in the medulla.
    - **Lateral Columns:** Contains the lateral corticospinal tract (motor control, decussates in the medulla) and the lateral spinothalamic tract (pain/temperature, decussates within 1-2 segments of entry).
    - **Ventral Columns (Anterior Funiculus):** Contains the anterior corticospinal tract (motor control, decussates at the level of entry) and the anterior spinothalamic tract (crude touch/pressure, decussates within 1-2 segments of entry).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient has motor paralysis and loss of pain/temperature below T4, indicating damage to the corticospinal tracts (both lateral and anterior) and spinothalamic tracts (both lateral and anterior). The preservation of proprioception, vibration, and fine touch below T4 indicates that the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus) are spared. The lesion is at C4-C5, which is above T4. Therefore, the tracts carrying these sensations from below the level of the lesion (T4 and below) must be intact. The fasciculus gracilis carries these sensations from the lower body and trunk, and the fasciculus cuneatus carries them from the upper body. Since both are spared, the dorsal columns are functioning.
- **Specific Tracts:**
    - **Fasciculus Gracilis:** Medial part of the dorsal column, carries proprioception, vibration, and fine touch from the lower limbs and lower trunk (below T6).
    - **Fasciculus Cuneatus:** Lateral part of the dorsal column, carries proprioception, vibration, and fine touch from the upper limbs and upper trunk (above T6).
    - **Lateral Corticospinal Tract:** Descends in the lateral column, controls voluntary movement, decussates in the medulla. Damage causes contralateral weakness below the lesion.
    - **Anterior Corticospinal Tract:** Descends in the anterior column, controls axial/trunk muscles, decussates at the spinal cord level. Damage causes ipsilateral weakness below the lesion.
    - **Spinothalamic Tract:** Ascends in the lateral and anterior columns, carries pain, temperature, crude touch, and pressure. Damage causes contralateral loss of these sensations below the lesion.
- **Conclusion:** The preservation of proprioception and fine touch below T4 points to the sparing of the dorsal columns, which consist of the fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus. Option (C) Fasciculus gracilis is part of the spared dorsal columns.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lateral corticospinal tract:** This tract carries motor commands and decussates in the medulla. Damage causes contralateral weakness and spasticity below the lesion. The patient has paralysis below T4, indicating damage to corticospinal tracts, but this tract is *damaged*, not spared.
- (B) **Anterior corticospinal tract:** This tract carries motor commands, primarily for axial muscles, and decussates at the spinal cord level. Damage causes ipsilateral weakness below the lesion. The patient has paralysis below T4, indicating damage to corticospinal tracts, but this tract is *damaged*, not spared.
- (D) **Fasciculus cuneatus:** This tract is part of the dorsal columns and carries proprioception, vibration, and fine touch from the upper limbs and upper trunk (above T6). While the dorsal columns are spared, the question asks which tract is *most likely* spared accounting for the preservation of proprioception and fine touch *below T4*. The fasciculus gracilis carries these sensations from the lower body and trunk (below T6), which is the primary area affected by the T4 sensory level. While fasciculus cuneatus is also spared, fasciculus gracilis is the more relevant tract for the sensory level described. However, both are part of the spared dorsal columns. The key is recognizing the dorsal columns are spared. Given the options, both C and D are part of the spared dorsal columns. But the sensory level is T4. Fasciculus gracilis carries sensation from below T6 (lower limbs/trunk), and fasciculus cuneatus carries sensation from above T6 (upper limbs/trunk). The sensory level at T4 implies the lower limbs and trunk sensation (carried by fasciculus gracilis) is preserved. Therefore, fasciculus gracilis is the most pertinent spared tract explaining the preserved sensation *below* the lesion level.
- (E) **Spinothalamic tract:** This tract carries pain and temperature sensation and decussates shortly after entering the spinal cord. Damage causes contralateral loss of pain and temperature below the lesion. The patient has lost pain and temperature sensation below T4, indicating damage to this tract, not sparing.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Fasciculus Gracilis (C) | Fasciculus Cuneatus (D) | Lateral Corticospinal Tract (A) | Anterior Corticospinal Tract (B) | Spinothalamic Tract (E) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Location**            | Dorsal Column (Medial)   | Dorsal Column (Lateral)  | Lateral Column                 | Ventral Column                 | Lateral/Ventral Column  |
| **Function**            | Proprioception, Vibration, Fine Touch | Proprioception, Vibration, Fine Touch | Motor Control                  | Motor Control (Axial)         | Pain, Temperature, Crude Touch |
| **Sensory Modality**    | Epicritic                | Epicritic                | Motor                          | Motor                          | Nociceptive/Thermoreceptive |
| **Decussation**         | Medulla                 | Medulla                 | Medulla                        | Spinal Cord Level              | Spinal Cord (1-2 segments) |
| **Level of Sensation**  | Below T6 (Lower Limb/Trunk) | Above T6 (Upper Limb/Trunk) | N/A                            | N/A                            | N/A                     |
| **Status in Vignette**  | Spared                  | Spared                  | Damaged                        | Damaged                        | Damaged                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The preservation of proprioception, vibration, and fine touch sensation below a spinal cord lesion level indicates sparing of the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus). Motor paralysis and loss of pain/temperature sensation indicate damage to the corticospinal tracts and spinothalamic tracts, respectively. Differentiating between these tracts based on clinical findings is crucial for localizing spinal cord lesions. Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (ipsilateral motor/proprioception loss, contralateral pain/temp loss) and complete cord transection (loss of all modalities below the lesion).

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 42 (Posterior)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between posterior column (dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway) and spinothalamic tract lesions. (基于临床表现定位脊髓损伤，特别是区分后索（背柱-内侧丘脑通路）和脊髓丘脑束损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall down a flight of stairs. He complains of severe neck pain and bilateral lower extremity weakness. On examination, his motor strength is 5/5 in the upper extremities but 2/5 in the lower extremities bilaterally. Sensory examination reveals complete loss of proprioception and vibration sense below the T10 dermatome bilaterally. Pain and temperature sensation are intact below T10 bilaterally. Reflexes are brisk (3+) in the lower extremities, and plantar responses are extensor bilaterally (Babinski sign positive). An MRI of the cervical spine reveals a complete transection of the spinal cord at the C6 level.

Which of the following sensory modalities would be expected to be *preserved* in this patient?

(A) Fine touch sensation in the fingertips
(B) Pain sensation in the left lower extremity
(C) Proprioception in the right ankle
(D) Temperature sensation in the right palm
(E) Vibration sense at the distal interphalangeal joint of the left thumb

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, fall, neck pain, bilateral lower extremity weakness. Suggests spinal cord injury.
- **Motor Exam:** Upper extremities 5/5, lower extremities 2/5. Indicates a lesion below the cervical enlargement (affecting upper limb motor neurons) but above the lumbar enlargement (affecting lower limb motor neurons).
- **Sensory Exam:** Loss of proprioception and vibration below T10. These modalities travel in the posterior (dorsal) columns (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus). The loss below T10 suggests the lesion is above T10.
- **Sensory Exam:** Pain and temperature sensation intact below T10. These modalities travel in the spinothalamic tract (anterolateral system). Intact sensation indicates the spinothalamic tract is spared *below* the level of the lesion.
- **Reflexes:** Brisk lower extremity reflexes (3+) and positive Babinski signs. Indicate upper motor neuron lesion, consistent with spinal cord injury.
- **MRI:** Complete transection at C6. This confirms a complete spinal cord injury at the C6 level.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which sensory modality would be *preserved*.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord is divided into dorsal (posterior) and ventral (anterior) horns, dorsal (posterior) columns, lateral columns, and ventral (anterior) columns.
- **Sensory Pathways:**
    - **Posterior Columns (Dorsal Column-Medial Lemniscus System):** Carries fine touch, vibration, proprioception, and two-point discrimination. Fibers enter the spinal cord, ascend ipsilaterally in the fasciculus gracilis (lower body) and fasciculus cuneatus (upper body), synapse in the gracile and cuneate nuclei in the medulla, decussate (cross) as the internal arcuate fibers, and ascend as the medial lemniscus to the thalamus (VPL nucleus).
    - **Spinothalamic Tract (Anterolateral System):** Carries pain, temperature, and crude touch. Fibers enter the spinal cord, synapse in the dorsal horn (substantia gelatinosa), decussate within 1-2 segments of entry, and ascend contralaterally in the anterolateral white matter to the thalamus (VPL nucleus).
- **Lesion at C6:** A complete transection at C6 will interrupt all ascending and descending tracts at that level.
    - **Posterior Columns:** Sensory information from below C6 (including proprioception, vibration, fine touch) will be lost *below* the level of the lesion.
    - **Spinothalamic Tract:** Sensory information from below C6 (including pain and temperature) will be lost *below* the level of the lesion. The decussation occurs within 1-2 segments, so the loss will be contralateral to the lesion level, but still below C6.
    - **Motor Tracts (Corticospinal):** Motor control from the brain will be lost below the level of the lesion (causing lower extremity weakness).
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - (A) Fine touch sensation in the fingertips: Travels via posterior columns. The lesion is at C6, which is above the sensory innervation for the fingertips. Therefore, fine touch sensation in the fingertips would be *lost*.
    - (B) Pain sensation in the left lower extremity: Travels via the spinothalamic tract. Fibers from the left lower extremity enter the spinal cord, synapse, decussate, and ascend contralaterally. The lesion is at C6. Since the spinothalamic tract decussates relatively quickly, the loss of pain sensation would be contralateral (right side) below the lesion. Therefore, pain sensation in the *left* lower extremity would be *preserved* because the fibers have already crossed over to the right side of the spinal cord *below* the C6 lesion level.
    - (C) Proprioception in the right ankle: Travels via the posterior columns. The lesion is at C6, which is above the sensory innervation for the right ankle. Therefore, proprioception in the right ankle would be *lost*.
    - (D) Temperature sensation in the right palm: Travels via the spinothalamic tract. The lesion is at C6, which is above the sensory innervation for the right palm. Therefore, temperature sensation in the right palm would be *lost*.
    - (E) Vibration sense at the distal interphalangeal joint of the left thumb: Travels via the posterior columns. The lesion is at C6, which is above the sensory innervation for the left thumb. Therefore, vibration sense in the left thumb would be *lost*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Fine touch travels in the posterior columns. A C6 lesion interrupts these fibers, leading to loss of fine touch below the lesion level, including the fingertips. Test-takers might confuse this with the spinothalamic tract.
- (C): Incorrect. Proprioception travels in the posterior columns. A C6 lesion interrupts these fibers, leading to loss of proprioception below the lesion level, including the right ankle. Test-takers might confuse this with the spinothalamic tract.
- (D): Incorrect. Temperature sensation travels in the spinothalamic tract. A C6 lesion interrupts these fibers, leading to loss of temperature sensation below the lesion level, including the right palm. Test-takers might forget the rapid decussation of the spinothalamic tract.
- (E): Incorrect. Vibration sense travels in the posterior columns. A C6 lesion interrupts these fibers, leading to loss of vibration sense below the lesion level, including the left thumb. Test-takers might confuse this with the spinothalamic tract.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Posterior Columns (Dorsal Column-Medial Lemniscus) | Spinothalamic Tract (Anterolateral System) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Modalities**       | Fine touch, vibration, proprioception, 2-pt disc. | Pain, temperature, crude touch             |
| **Fiber Course**     | Ascend ipsilaterally, decussate in medulla.      | Decussate in spinal cord (1-2 segments), ascend contralaterally. |
| **Location**         | Posterior funiculi (fasciculus gracilis/cuneatus) | Anterolateral white matter                |
| **Lesion Effect**    | Loss ipsilateral below lesion.                   | Loss contralateral below lesion.           |
| **C6 Lesion Effect** | Loss of modalities ipsilateral below C6.         | Loss of modalities contralateral below C6. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A complete spinal cord transection interrupts all ascending and descending tracts at the level of the lesion. Sensory modalities traveling in the posterior columns (fine touch, vibration, proprioception) are lost ipsilaterally below the lesion. Sensory modalities traveling in the spinothalamic tract (pain, temperature) are lost contralaterally below the lesion due to their rapid decussation within the spinal cord. Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and incomplete cord injuries.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 43 (intermediate)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical organization of the spinal cord, specifically the location and function of the lateral corticospinal tract, and correlating a clinical presentation of contralateral hemiparesis with a specific spinal cord lesion level. (理解脊髓的解剖组织，特别是侧皮质脊髓束的位置和功能，并将对侧半身不遂的临床表现与特定的脊髓损伤水平相关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a motor vehicle accident. He complains of weakness and numbness on his right side, which started immediately after the accident. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right-sided hemiparesis (muscle strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of his body below the level of the T4 dermatome, and an absent right Achilles reflex. Motor strength is 5/5 on the left side. Sensation is intact on the left side. Babinski sign is present on the right side. MRI of the thoracic spine shows a focal lesion compressing the left side of the spinal cord at the T4 vertebral level.

Which tract is most likely affected by this lesion, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Anterior corticospinal tract
(B) Lateral corticospinal tract
(C) Spinothalamic tract
(D) Dorsal columns
(E) Ventral horn cells

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, MVA, acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. This points to an acute neurological event affecting the motor and sensory pathways.
- **Neurological Examination:**
    - Right-sided hemiparesis (weakness): Indicates damage to the motor pathway controlling the right side of the body.
    - Decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side below T4: Indicates damage to the sensory pathway carrying pain and temperature sensation from the right side of the body below T4.
    - Absent right Achilles reflex: Suggests damage to the reflex arc involving the spinal cord segment controlling the lower limb (L5-S1), potentially affecting the afferent or efferent limb or the interneuron within the spinal cord.
    - Babinski sign present on the right: Indicates upper motor neuron lesion affecting the right lower limb.
- **MRI Findings:** Focal lesion compressing the *left* side of the spinal cord at the T4 vertebral level.
- **Localization:** The lesion is at T4 on the *left* side. The symptoms are on the *right* side below T4. This pattern (lesion on one side of the cord causing contralateral symptoms below the lesion level) is characteristic of damage to descending tracts that cross in the medulla (like the corticospinal tract) and ascending tracts that cross in the spinal cord (like the spinothalamic tract). The lesion affects the right side of the body because the left corticospinal tract controls the right side, and the left spinothalamic tract carries sensation from the right side. The lesion is at T4, explaining why symptoms are below this level.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord is organized into gray matter (central H-shape) and white matter (surrounding gray matter). White matter contains ascending (sensory) and descending (motor) tracts.
- **Corticospinal Tract:** This is the primary descending motor pathway. It originates in the motor cortex, descends through the internal capsule, brainstem (where most fibers cross in the medulla to form the lateral corticospinal tract), and into the spinal cord.
    - **Lateral Corticospinal Tract:** Located in the lateral white matter column. It controls voluntary movement of the limbs and trunk. Fibers cross in the medulla (pyramidal decussation) and synapse on interneurons and motor neurons in the ventral horn of the spinal cord. A lesion on the *left* side of the spinal cord at T4 will affect the *left* lateral corticospinal tract, which carries motor commands for the *right* side of the body below the level of the lesion. This explains the right-sided hemiparesis and positive Babinski sign (upper motor neuron sign).
    - **Anterior (Ventral) Corticospinal Tract:** A smaller portion of corticospinal fibers descends ipsilaterally in the anterior white matter column and crosses near the spinal cord segment it innervates. It primarily controls axial muscles. Damage here would cause ipsilateral weakness, which is not seen in this patient.
- **Spinothalamic Tract:** This is the primary ascending pathway for pain and temperature sensation. Fibers enter the spinal cord, synapse in the dorsal horn, cross to the contralateral side within a few segments (anterior white commissure), and ascend in the lateral white matter column to the thalamus. A lesion on the *left* side of the spinal cord at T4 will affect the *left* spinothalamic tract, which carries pain and temperature sensation from the *right* side of the body below the level of the lesion. This explains the decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side below T4.
- **Dorsal Columns:** These ascending pathways (fasciculus gracilis and fasciculus cuneatus) carry fine touch, vibration, and proprioception. Fibers enter the spinal cord, ascend ipsilaterally in the posterior white matter column, and cross in the medulla. A lesion at T4 would affect the *left* dorsal columns, causing loss of fine touch, vibration, and proprioception on the *left* side of the body below T4. This is not the primary finding in this patient.
- **Ventral Horn Cells:** These are the lower motor neurons located in the anterior gray matter. They send axons out via the ventral roots to innervate skeletal muscles. Damage to ventral horn cells causes flaccid paralysis, muscle atrophy, and fasciculations *ipsilaterally* at the level of the lesion. This patient has upper motor neuron signs (hemiparesis, Babinski) contralateral to the lesion, not lower motor neuron signs ipsilaterally.

Therefore, the combination of contralateral hemiparesis (right side) and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation (right side) below the T4 level, caused by a lesion on the left side of the spinal cord at T4, points to damage involving the left lateral corticospinal tract and the left spinothalamic tract. The question asks which tract is most likely affected leading to the patient's presentation, and the contralateral hemiparesis is a hallmark sign of lateral corticospinal tract involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Anterior corticospinal tract:** Incorrect. This tract descends ipsilaterally and crosses near the spinal cord segment. Damage would cause *ipsilateral* weakness, primarily affecting axial muscles. The patient has *contralateral* weakness affecting limbs and trunk.
- (C) **Spinothalamic tract:** Incorrect. While the patient *does* have symptoms related to spinothalamic tract damage (contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation), the question asks which tract is most likely affected *leading to the patient's clinical presentation*. The contralateral hemiparesis is a more prominent feature and is specifically caused by the lateral corticospinal tract. Furthermore, the question implies a single tract is primarily responsible, although multiple tracts are affected by the lesion. The corticospinal tract is the primary motor pathway.
- (D) **Dorsal columns:** Incorrect. These tracts carry fine touch, vibration, and proprioception. They ascend ipsilaterally and cross in the medulla. Damage at T4 would cause loss of these sensations on the *left* side below T4. The patient's primary sensory deficit is pain and temperature (spinothalamic) on the right side.
- (E) **Ventral horn cells:** Incorrect. These are lower motor neurons. Damage causes flaccid paralysis, atrophy, and fasciculations *ipsilaterally* at the level of the lesion. The patient has upper motor neuron signs (spasticity implied by hemiparesis, positive Babinski) *contralaterally*.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Lateral Corticospinal Tract | Anterior Corticospinal Tract | Spinothalamic Tract | Dorsal Columns | Ventral Horn Cells |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------------ | :------------- | :----------------- |
| **Function**            | Voluntary Motor Control    | Axial Motor Control         | Pain & Temperature  | Fine Touch, Proprioception, Vibration | Lower Motor Neurons |
| **Origin**              | Motor Cortex               | Motor Cortex                | Spinal Cord Dorsal Horn | Spinal Cord Dorsal Horn | Spinal Cord Gray Matter |
| **Location (Spinal Cord)** | Lateral White Matter       | Anterior White Matter       | Lateral White Matter | Posterior White Matter | Anterior Gray Matter |
| **Crossing**            | Medulla (Pyramidal Decussation) | Spinal Cord Segment Level | Spinal Cord (1-2 segments) | Medulla        | N/A                |
| **Lesion Effect (T4 Left)** | Right Hemiparesis (UMN)   | Left Ipsilateral Weakness (Axial) | Left Contralateral Pain/Temp Loss | Left Ipsilateral Loss of Fine Touch/Proprioception | Left Ipsilateral Flaccid Paralysis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A lesion on one side of the spinal cord (hemisection) typically affects the ipsilateral dorsal columns, ventral horn cells, and the contralateral corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts below the level of the lesion. This results in ipsilateral loss of fine touch, vibration, and proprioception, ipsilateral lower motor neuron signs (if ventral horn cells are involved), and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation and upper motor neuron signs (hemiparesis). Key differentials include complete spinal cord transection (bilateral deficits) and Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection).

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 44 (sulcus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the embryological development of the spinal cord, specifically the formation of the median sulcus, and its clinical significance in congenital anomalies like spina bifida. (理解脊髓的胚胎发育，特别是脊髓中槽的形成及其在脊髓膨出等先天性异常中的临床意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-day-old male infant is brought to the emergency department by his parents due to a visible sac on his lower back. The infant was born full-term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery. Prenatal screening was unremarkable. Physical examination reveals a 5 cm x 3 cm cystic mass overlying the lumbosacral spine, covered by thin, translucent skin. Neurological examination shows absent deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities, bilateral foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot), and decreased sensation below the level of the lesion (T10 dermatome). An MRI of the lumbar spine confirms a meningocele associated with a defect in the posterior vertebral arches at L5-S1. The spinal cord itself appears normal in morphology and position within the spinal canal, without evidence of tethering or lipomyelomeningocele. The lesion is characterized by a prominent, deep groove running along the midline of the exposed spinal cord within the meningocele sac, visible on the MRI.

Which of the following structures is most likely represented by the prominent midline groove observed on the MRI?

(A) Filum terminale
(B) Dorsal median sulcus
(C) Ventral median fissure
(D) Intermediate zone of the spinal cord
(E) Central canal

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Newborn with a visible sac on the lower back (meningocele).
- **Physical Exam:** Absent lower extremity reflexes, foot drop, decreased sensation below T10. These findings indicate a lower motor neuron lesion affecting the lumbosacral spinal cord segments.
- **Imaging (MRI):** Confirms meningocele at L5-S1. The spinal cord is normal in morphology and position, ruling out lipomyelomeningocele or significant tethering. Crucially, the MRI shows a prominent midline groove on the *exposed* spinal cord within the meningocele.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the anatomical structure corresponding to this prominent midline groove.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Embryology of the Spinal Cord:** The neural tube forms from the ectoderm during neurulation. The anterior (ventral) part folds towards the midline, and the posterior (dorsal) part folds towards the midline.
- **Formation of Sulci/Fissures:**
    - **Median Sulcus (Dorsal):** The dorsal folds meet in the midline, but they do not fuse completely. This incomplete fusion creates a shallow groove on the *posterior* surface of the spinal cord called the dorsal median sulcus. It is typically located between the dorsal horns.
    - **Median Fissure (Ventral):** The ventral folds fuse completely in the midline, forming a deep groove on the *anterior* surface of the spinal cord called the ventral median fissure. It separates the two ventral horns.
- **Clinical Correlation:** In cases of spina bifida aperta (like meningocele or myelomeningocele), the failure of the vertebral arches to close exposes the underlying neural tissue and meninges. If the dorsal median sulcus is prominent and exposed, it appears as a midline groove on the surface of the spinal cord within the sac. The description specifies a groove on the *exposed* spinal cord, which corresponds to the dorsal surface.
- **Distinguishing Sulcus vs. Fissure:** The dorsal median sulcus is a shallow groove on the *posterior* surface, while the ventral median fissure is a deep groove on the *anterior* surface. The question describes a groove on the *exposed* spinal cord in a meningocele, which is located dorsally.
- **Other Options:**
    - **Filum terminale:** A fibrous extension of the pia mater that anchors the spinal cord to the coccyx. It is located at the caudal end of the spinal cord, not a midline groove along the cord's surface.
    - **Intermediate zone:** The region between the dorsal and ventral horns, containing interneurons. It is not a distinct external groove.
    - **Central canal:** The channel running longitudinally through the center of the spinal cord, lined by ependymal cells. It is internal, not an external groove.

##### 3. Embryological Origin & Clinical Significance (胚胎发生起源与临床意义)
- The dorsal median sulcus represents the site where the dorsal neural folds failed to completely fuse during embryogenesis. In spina bifida, this incomplete fusion is externally visible as a groove or cleft on the dorsal surface of the spinal cord, especially when the overlying meninges and neural tissue are exposed.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Filum terminale:** This is a fibrous strand extending from the conus medullaris to the coccyx. It is located at the inferior end of the spinal cord, not a midline groove along the surface. It is involved in tethered cord syndrome but is not the structure described. Test-takers might confuse it with structures at the caudal end of the spinal cord.
- (C) **Ventral median fissure:** This is a deep groove on the *anterior* (ventral) surface of the spinal cord, formed by the complete fusion of the ventral neural folds. The clinical vignette describes a groove on the *exposed* spinal cord in a meningocele, which is located on the *posterior* (dorsal) surface. Test-takers might confuse the terms "sulcus" and "fissure" or forget the location.
- (D) **Intermediate zone of the spinal cord:** This is a region of gray matter located between the dorsal and ventral horns, containing interneurons. It is an internal structure, not a visible external groove. Test-takers might incorrectly associate it with the gray matter region.
- (E) **Central canal:** This is the cerebrospinal fluid-filled channel running through the center of the spinal cord. It is an internal structure, not a visible external groove. Test-takers might confuse it with the midline structures of the spinal cord.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location          | Appearance                                  | Embryological Origin                                    | Clinical Relevance                                                                 |
|----------------------|-------------------|---------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Dorsal Median Sulcus** | Posterior Surface | Shallow groove, between dorsal horns          | Incomplete fusion of dorsal neural folds                 | Visible groove in spina bifida aperta (meningocele/myelomeningocele) on dorsal surface |
| Ventral Median Fissure | Anterior Surface  | Deep groove, separates ventral horns         | Complete fusion of ventral neural folds                  | Internal landmark, separates motor neurons                                         |
| Filum Terminale      | Caudal End        | Fibrous strand                               | Extension of pia mater                                   | Tethered cord syndrome                                                              |
| Central Canal         | Center            | CSF-filled channel                            | Lumen of the neural tube                                 | CSF circulation                                                                    |
| Intermediate Zone     | Internal          | Gray matter region between horns              | Neural tube differentiation                              | Contains interneurons                                                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The dorsal median sulcus is a shallow groove on the posterior surface of the spinal cord resulting from the incomplete fusion of the dorsal neural folds during embryogenesis. It becomes clinically apparent as a midline groove on the exposed spinal cord in cases of spina bifida aperta, such as meningocele. Differentiating it from the ventral median fissure (anterior surface) and internal structures like the central canal and filum terminale is crucial. Key differentials include ventral median fissure (anterior location, deep groove), central canal (internal), and filum terminale (caudal end).

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 45 (Posterior)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of a spinal cord lesion based on specific sensory and motor deficits, understanding the anatomical organization of the posterior spinal cord (dorsal columns, posterior horns, posterior funiculi) and its relationship to specific tracts and nuclei, and differentiating between various spinal cord syndromes. (基于特定感觉和运动功能障碍定位脊髓损伤，理解脊髓后部（背索、后角、后索）的解剖组织及其与特定束和核的关系，以及区分各种脊髓综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall down a flight of stairs. He complains of severe neck pain and bilateral upper extremity weakness. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals complete paralysis of all voluntary muscles below the level of C5 bilaterally. Sensory examination shows loss of proprioception and vibration sense below C5 bilaterally, but preservation of pain and temperature sensation bilaterally. Light touch sensation is also intact bilaterally below C5. Motor examination confirms flaccid paralysis of biceps, triceps, wrist extensors, and finger flexors bilaterally. Reflexes are absent in the upper extremities. A spinal MRI reveals a complete transection of the spinal cord at the C5 vertebral level.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely spared in this patient's spinal cord injury, accounting for the preservation of pain and temperature sensation below the level of the lesion?

(A) Fasciculus gracilis
(B) Fasciculus cuneatus
(C) Lateral spinothalamic tract
(D) Anterior spinothalamic tract
(E) Dorsal column nuclei

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, fall, neck pain, bilateral upper extremity weakness. Suggests cervical spinal cord injury.
- **Neurological Exam:**
    - Complete paralysis below C5 bilaterally: Indicates a complete lesion affecting motor pathways (corticospinal tracts) at or below C5.
    - Loss of proprioception and vibration sense below C5 bilaterally: Points to damage to the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus).
    - Preservation of pain and temperature sensation below C5 bilaterally: This is the crucial finding. It indicates that the pathways carrying these modalities are intact.
    - Intact light touch sensation: Light touch is carried by both dorsal columns and spinothalamic tracts, but its preservation here is less specific than the preserved pain/temperature.
- **MRI Findings:** Complete transection at C5. Confirms a severe spinal cord injury at the C5 level.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is MOST likely spared, explaining the preserved pain/temperature sensation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Spinal Cord Anatomy:** The spinal cord is organized into posterior (dorsal), lateral, and anterior (ventral) columns.
    - **Posterior Column (Dorsal Funiculus):** Contains the fasciculus gracilis (medial lemniscus pathway) carrying fine touch, proprioception, and vibration from the lower body, and the fasciculus cuneatus (medial lemniscus pathway) carrying the same modalities from the upper body. These tracts ascend ipsilaterally and synapse in the dorsal column nuclei (nucleus gracilis and nucleus cuneatus) in the medulla. The question stem describes loss of proprioception and vibration below C5, indicating damage to the dorsal columns (specifically fasciculus cuneatus for upper body sensation, and potentially fasciculus gracilis if the lesion extended lower, though the primary deficit described is upper extremity).
    - **Lateral Column:** Contains the lateral spinothalamic tract (LSTT) carrying pain and temperature sensation contralaterally, and the anterior spinothalamic tract (ASTT) carrying crude touch and pressure contralaterally. It also contains the corticospinal tracts (motor).
    - **Anterior Column (Ventral Funiculus):** Contains the anterior spinothalamic tract (ASTT) and the corticospinal tracts.
- **Clinical Correlation:**
    - Loss of motor function below C5 indicates damage to the corticospinal tracts, which are located primarily in the lateral and anterior columns.
    - Loss of proprioception and vibration below C5 indicates damage to the dorsal columns (fasciculus cuneatus for upper limbs).
    - Preservation of pain and temperature sensation below C5 indicates that the spinothalamic tracts (specifically the lateral spinothalamic tract, which carries the majority of pain and temperature) are intact. The LSTT decussates within 1-2 segments of entering the spinal cord, so a lesion at C5 would spare the LSTT carrying pain/temperature from below C5.
- **Evaluating Options:**
    - (A) Fasciculus gracilis: Part of the dorsal column, carries proprioception/vibration from the lower body. Likely damaged if the lesion extends below T6, but the primary deficit described is upper limb sensory loss.
    - (B) Fasciculus cuneatus: Part of the dorsal column, carries proprioception/vibration from the upper body. Damaged, explaining the loss of proprioception/vibration below C5.
    - (C) Lateral spinothalamic tract: Carries pain and temperature sensation contralaterally. Preserved, explaining the intact pain and temperature sensation below C5.
    - (D) Anterior spinothalamic tract: Carries crude touch and pressure contralaterally. Located in the anterior column. Damage to this tract would cause loss of crude touch/pressure, but the vignette states light touch is intact, and the key finding is preserved pain/temperature. The corticospinal tracts are also in the anterior column, which are damaged (explaining motor loss).
    - (E) Dorsal column nuclei: Located in the medulla, where the fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus synapse. The lesion is at C5, so these nuclei are spared. However, the question asks which *structure* is spared *in the spinal cord* to explain the preserved sensation. The tracts themselves (fasciculus gracilis/cuneatus) are damaged, leading to the sensory loss. The nuclei are downstream and therefore spared by the C5 lesion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Fasciculus gracilis: Incorrect. This tract carries proprioception and vibration from the lower limbs and lower trunk. While the lesion is at C5, damage to the dorsal columns (including fasciculus cuneatus for upper limbs) is evident from the loss of proprioception/vibration below C5. If the lesion extended lower, fasciculus gracilis would also be affected. However, the preservation of pain/temperature points to sparing of the spinothalamic tracts, not the dorsal columns.
- (B) Fasciculus cuneatus: Incorrect. This tract carries proprioception and vibration from the upper limbs and upper trunk. The patient has lost these sensations below C5, indicating damage to this tract.
- (D) Anterior spinothalamic tract: Incorrect. This tract carries crude touch and pressure. While located in the anterior column (along with corticospinal tracts, which are damaged), the preservation of light touch sensation makes significant damage to this tract less likely as the primary explanation for the sensory findings. More importantly, the key preserved sensation is pain and temperature, carried by the *lateral* spinothalamic tract.
- (E) Dorsal column nuclei: Incorrect. These nuclei are located in the medulla oblongata, not the spinal cord at C5. While they are spared by a C5 lesion, the question asks about structures *within* the spinal cord that explain the preserved sensation. The dorsal column tracts (fasciculus cuneatus) *within* the spinal cord are damaged.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location             | Function                                     | Status in C5 Transection | Explanation for Clinical Findings                                                                                                                               |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Fasciculus Gracilis           | Posterior Column     | Proprioception/Vibration (Lower Body)        | Likely Damaged           | Explains loss of proprioception/vibration from lower body *if* lesion extends below T6. Not the primary explanation for upper limb deficits.                     |
| Fasciculus Cuneatus           | Posterior Column     | Proprioception/Vibration (Upper Body)        | Damaged                 | Explains loss of proprioception/vibration in upper extremities below C5.                                                                                         |
| Lateral Spinothalamic Tract    | Lateral Column       | Pain, Temperature (Contralateral)            | Spared                  | Explains preservation of pain and temperature sensation below C5.                                                                                                |
| Anterior Spinothalamic Tract   | Anterior Column      | Crude Touch, Pressure (Contralateral)       | Likely Damaged           | Located with corticospinal tracts; damage explains motor loss. Preservation of light touch makes significant damage less likely as the primary sensory explanation. |
| Corticospinal Tracts          | Lateral & Anterior Columns | Voluntary Motor Control                      | Damaged                 | Explains paralysis below C5.                                                                                                                                  |
| Dorsal Column Nuclei          | Medulla Oblongata    | Synapse for Fasciculus Gracilis & Cuneatus | Spared                  | Located downstream from C5 lesion; not directly responsible for sensory input *at* C5.                                                                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A complete spinal cord transection spares only the structures located *outside* the damaged area. In this case, the lesion damages the dorsal columns (causing loss of proprioception/vibration) and the corticospinal tracts (causing motor paralysis). The spinothalamic tracts, particularly the lateral spinothalamic tract carrying pain and temperature, are located more centrally and are spared, explaining the preservation of these sensations. Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and incomplete cord injuries, which would present differently.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 46 (median)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of spinal cord lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically differentiating between anterior spinal artery syndrome and Brown-Séquard syndrome, understanding the vascular supply and functional organization of the spinal cord. (根据临床表现定位脊髓损伤，区分前脊动脉综合征和Brown-Séquard综合征，理解脊髓的血管供应和功能组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe lower back pain followed by bilateral leg weakness and sensory loss. On examination, his blood pressure is 180/100 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals complete paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the T10 level bilaterally. Proprioception and vibration sense are intact below T10. Upper extremity motor and sensory function are normal. An MRI of the thoracic spine shows an acute infarction involving the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at the T10 level.

Which of the following vascular territories is most likely affected, leading to this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Posterior spinal arteries
(B) Anterior spinal artery
(C) Vertebral arteries
(D) Radicular arteries
(E) Vas anterior spinalis

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This suggests a vascular etiology for the neurological deficit.
*   **Onset:** Sudden onset of lower back pain followed by neurological deficits. This points towards an acute event like infarction or hemorrhage.
*   **Level:** Symptoms below T10 level. This localizes the lesion to the spinal cord at or above the T10 segment.
*   **Motor Deficits:** Bilateral leg weakness (paralysis). This indicates involvement of the corticospinal tracts.
*   **Sensory Deficits:** Loss of pain and temperature sensation below T10 bilaterally. This indicates involvement of the spinothalamic tracts.
*   **Sensory Preservation:** Intact proprioception and vibration sense below T10. This indicates preservation of the dorsal columns.
*   **Imaging:** MRI shows acute infarction involving the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord at T10. This confirms the vascular etiology and the location/extent of the lesion.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the vascular territory responsible.

The clinical picture describes a classic presentation of **Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome**. This syndrome results from occlusion of the anterior spinal artery or one of its major branches. The anterior spinal artery supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, which includes the anterior horns (motor neurons), the lateral corticospinal tracts (motor function), and the lateral spinothalamic tracts (pain and temperature sensation). The posterior columns (dorsal columns), responsible for proprioception and vibration, are supplied by the posterior spinal arteries and are typically spared in this syndrome. The MRI findings directly correlate with this clinical presentation, showing infarction in the anterior two-thirds of the cord.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
*   **Spinal Cord Vascular Supply:** The spinal cord receives blood supply from the anterior spinal artery (ASA), the posterior spinal arteries (PSA), and radicular arteries.
    *   **Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA):** Arises from the vertebral arteries, runs down the anterior median fissure of the spinal cord. It supplies the anterior two-thirds of the cord, including the anterior horns (alpha motor neurons), lateral corticospinal tracts (motor), and lateral spinothalamic tracts (pain/temperature). It also supplies the substantia gelatinosa.
    *   **Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSA):** Arise from the vertebral arteries or the posterior inferior cerebellar arteries (PICA), run along the posterolateral sulci. They supply the posterior one-third of the cord, including the dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus - proprioception/vibration) and the posterior horns.
    *   **Radicular Arteries:** Arise from segmental branches of the vertebral, ascending cervical, deep cervical, intercostal, lumbar, and sacral arteries. They penetrate the spinal cord at the nerve root entry zones and contribute to the ASA and PSA, providing collateral circulation.
*   **Functional Organization:**
    *   **Anterior Horns:** Contain alpha motor neurons (LMNs) innervating skeletal muscles.
    *   **Lateral Corticospinal Tracts:** Descending motor pathways originating in the cerebral cortex, controlling voluntary movement (UMNs).
    *   **Lateral Spinothalamic Tracts:** Ascending sensory pathways carrying pain and temperature information.
    *   **Dorsal Columns:** Ascending sensory pathways carrying proprioception, vibration, and fine touch information.
*   **Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome:** Occlusion of the ASA leads to infarction of the anterior two-thirds of the cord. Clinically, this manifests as bilateral loss of motor function (paralysis), pain, and temperature sensation below the level of the lesion, with preservation of proprioception and vibration sense. This matches the patient's presentation perfectly.
*   **Brown-Séquard Syndrome:** Hemisection of the spinal cord. Causes ipsilateral motor loss and loss of proprioception/vibration, and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation below the lesion. This does not match the patient's bilateral symptoms and preserved dorsal column function.
*   **Vas Anterior Spinalis:** This is an older term sometimes used synonymously with the anterior spinal artery, but "anterior spinal artery" is the preferred and more precise term.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
The spinal cord develops from the neural tube, which forms from the ectoderm. The anterior spinal artery develops as a longitudinal vessel within the ventral median sulcus of the developing spinal cord.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Posterior spinal arteries:** These supply the posterior one-third of the spinal cord, including the dorsal columns. Occlusion would cause loss of proprioception and vibration sense, which are preserved in this patient. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (C) **Vertebral arteries:** These are major arteries supplying the brainstem and cerebellum, and they give rise to the anterior and posterior spinal arteries. Occlusion of the vertebral arteries *could* lead to spinal cord infarction, but it would typically affect both anterior and posterior territories, or cause brainstem symptoms. The specific clinical picture (anterior 2/3 cord involvement) points directly to the ASA, which arises from the vertebral arteries. This option is too proximal and doesn't specify the vessel directly supplying the affected cord territory.
- (D) **Radicular arteries:** These are segmental arteries that contribute to the ASA and PSA. While occlusion of a radicular artery *could* cause a localized infarct, it's less likely to cause a widespread infarction affecting the entire anterior two-thirds of the cord at a specific level like the ASA occlusion does. Furthermore, the clinical syndrome described is characteristic of ASA territory involvement.
- (E) **Vas anterior spinalis:** This is an older, less commonly used term for the anterior spinal artery. While technically referring to the same vessel, option (B) uses the standard, preferred terminology. In a USMLE context, the standard term is expected. Furthermore, even if considered synonymous, the analysis for (B) remains the correct explanation for the clinical syndrome.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Anterior Spinal Artery (ASA) | Posterior Spinal Arteries (PSA) | Vertebral Arteries | Radicular Arteries |
| :------------------ | :--------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :----------------- | :----------------- |
| **Territory**       | Anterior 2/3 of cord         | Posterior 1/3 of cord          | Brainstem/Cerebellum, origin of ASA/PSA | Segmental supply to cord |
| **Structures Supplied** | Motor tracts, Spinothalamic | Dorsal Columns                | Proximal supply    | Contributes to ASA/PSA |
| **Clinical Syndrome (Occlusion)** | Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome (Motor, Pain/Temp loss, preserved Proprioception/Vibration) | Posterior Column Syndrome (Proprioception/Vibration loss) | Variable, often brainstem signs + spinal cord signs | Localized infarct |
| **Patient Presentation** | Bilateral motor/pain/temp loss below T10, preserved proprioception/vibration | Loss of proprioception/vibration below lesion | Often involves brainstem symptoms | Less common cause of this specific syndrome |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of bilateral motor weakness and loss of pain/temperature sensation with preserved proprioception/vibration below a specific spinal level is characteristic of Anterior Spinal Artery Syndrome. This syndrome results from occlusion of the anterior spinal artery, which supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord. Key differentials include Brown-Séquard syndrome (hemisection) and posterior spinal artery syndrome. Always correlate clinical findings with the known vascular territories and functional organization of the spinal cord.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 47 (sulcus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the embryological development of the spinal cord, specifically the formation of the median sulcus and its clinical significance in congenital anomalies like spina bifida occulta and myelomeningocele. (理解脊髓的胚胎发育，特别是脊髓中槽的形成及其在脊髓膨出等先天性异常中的临床意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is brought to the pediatrician by her mother for a routine check-up. During the physical examination, a tuft of hair is noted overlying the lower lumbar spine. Palpation reveals a subcutaneous mass approximately 5 cm in diameter, which is soft and pulsatile. Neurological examination shows absent deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities and decreased sensation below the level of the lesion. An MRI of the lumbar spine confirms a meningocele extending through a defect in the vertebral arches, containing cerebrospinal fluid and meninges, but not neural tissue. The defect is located at the L5 vertebral level. Which of the following structures is most likely involved in the embryological failure leading to this condition?

(A) Failure of the neural tube to close completely, resulting in incomplete fusion of the vertebral arches.
(B) Failure of the somites to properly segment, leading to vertebral agenesis.
(C) Failure of the notochord to induce neural tube closure, resulting in a persistent median sulcus.
(D) Failure of the neural crest cells to migrate properly, leading to Hirschsprung disease.
(E) Failure of the sclerotome to differentiate into cartilage, leading to hemivertebrae.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 3-day-old female infant.
- **Presentation:** Tuft of hair overlying lower lumbar spine, subcutaneous soft pulsatile mass (5 cm), absent DTRs in lower extremities, decreased sensation below lesion.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms meningocele through vertebral arch defect at L5.
- **Diagnosis:** This clinical picture is classic for myelomeningocele (although the description specifies a meningocele, the presence of a tuft of hair, neurological deficits, and vertebral defect strongly points towards a spectrum of neural tube defects including myelomeningocele, where the spinal cord itself is involved). The key is the defect in the vertebral arches and the protrusion of meninges (and potentially neural tissue/spinal cord).
- **Question:** Asks for the embryological failure leading to this condition.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Embryology of Spinal Cord & Vertebrae:** The spinal cord develops from the neural tube, which forms during neurulation. The neural tube folds and fuses, starting in the cervical region and proceeding cranially and caudally. The vertebral column develops from the sclerotome, a part of the somite. The notochord plays a crucial role in inducing the overlying ectoderm to form the neural plate and neural tube.
- **Neural Tube Defects:** Failure of the neural tube to close properly results in neural tube defects (NTDs). The most common NTDs are spina bifida (affecting the spinal cord) and anencephaly (affecting the brain).
- **Spina Bifida:** This term encompasses a range of defects.
    - **Spina Bifida Occulta:** A mild form where there is a defect in one or more vertebral arches, but the spinal cord and meninges remain within the vertebral canal. Often asymptomatic, may present with a skin dimple, tuft of hair, or lipoma.
    - **Meningocele:** Protrusion of the meninges through a defect in the vertebral arches.
    - **Myelomeningocele:** Protrusion of the spinal cord and meninges through a defect in the vertebral arches. This is the most severe form and is associated with significant neurological deficits.
- **Mechanism:** The clinical vignette describes a defect in the vertebral arches (L5) with protrusion of meninges (meningocele) and associated neurological deficits (absent reflexes, decreased sensation). This points to a failure of the neural tube to close completely during embryogenesis, leading to incomplete fusion of the vertebral arches. The neural tube fails to fuse posteriorly, leaving a gap through which the meninges (and potentially neural tissue) can herniate. The tuft of hair is a common cutaneous marker associated with underlying spina bifida.
- **Option (A):** Directly describes the embryological failure (incomplete neural tube closure) leading to the vertebral arch defect and subsequent herniation of meninges/spinal cord. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation of myelomeningocele/meningocele.
- **Option (B):** Failure of somite segmentation leads to vertebral agenesis (missing vertebrae) or fusion, not typically the type of arch defect seen in spina bifida.
- **Option (C):** Failure of the notochord to induce neural tube closure would prevent the neural tube from forming at all, which is incompatible with life, not a localized defect like spina bifida. A persistent median sulcus is a normal feature of the developing neural tube before closure, not a pathological failure.
- **Option (D):** Failure of neural crest cell migration leads to conditions like Hirschsprung disease (aganglionosis of the colon) or neuroblastoma, not spina bifida.
- **Option (E):** Failure of sclerotome differentiation leads to cartilage abnormalities like hemivertebrae (wedge-shaped vertebrae), which can be associated with scoliosis but are not the primary cause of spina bifida.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This accurately describes the primary embryological defect in neural tube defects like spina bifida, leading to the vertebral arch defect and herniation.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Somite segmentation defects cause different vertebral anomalies (e.g., agenesis, fusion, segmentation defects), not the specific type of arch defect seen in spina bifida. The primary issue in spina bifida is neural tube closure failure.
- (C): **Incorrect.** The notochord's role is crucial for *inducing* neural tube formation. Its failure would prevent the neural tube from forming initially. A persistent median sulcus is a normal developmental stage before fusion, not a failure. Spina bifida is a failure of *fusion* after the tube has formed.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Neural crest cells give rise to peripheral nervous system components, melanocytes, adrenal medulla cells, craniofacial cartilage/bone, etc. Their migration failure causes conditions like Hirschsprung disease, Waardenburg syndrome, or neurocristomas, unrelated to spinal cord closure.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Sclerotome differentiates into vertebrae. Failure leads to vertebral body or arch abnormalities (e.g., hemivertebrae, butterfly vertebrae). While vertebral abnormalities can occur *with* spina bifida, the *primary* embryological defect causing the herniation is neural tube closure failure, not a primary sclerotome differentiation failure.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Spina Bifida (Neural Tube Closure Failure) | Somite Segmentation Defect | Notochord Failure | Neural Crest Failure | Sclerotome Failure |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|-------------------|-----------------------|--------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Incomplete neural tube fusion           | Abnormal somite division   | No neural tube induction | Defective cell migration | Defective cartilage/bone formation |
| **Resulting Structure**| Vertebral arch defect, meningocele/myelomeningocele | Vertebral agenesis/fusion | No neural tube    | Hirschsprung, Neuroblastoma | Hemivertebrae, Butterfly vertebrae |
| **Embryological Origin**| Neural tube (neuroectoderm)             | Somites (mesoderm)          | Notochord (mesoderm) | Neural crest (ectoderm) | Sclerotome (mesoderm) |
| **Clinical Manifestation**| Neurological deficits, skin markers     | Scoliosis, spinal deformity | Incompatible with life | Bowel dysfunction, pigmentation issues | Scoliosis, spinal deformity |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary embryological defect in spina bifida (including meningocele and myelomeningocele) is the failure of the neural tube to close completely during the 4th week of gestation. This leads to a persistent defect in the vertebral arches, allowing herniation of meninges and/or spinal cord. Differentiate this from defects in somite segmentation (vertebral agenesis/fusion), notochord function (failure of neural tube induction), neural crest migration (Hirschsprung), or sclerotome differentiation (hemivertebrae).

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Neuroscience: The Spinal Cord: Part 3 Focus 48 (Posterior)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of a spinal cord lesion based on specific sensory and motor deficits, understanding the anatomical organization of the posterior spinal cord (dorsal columns, posterior horns, posterior funiculi) and its relationship to specific tracts and nuclei, and differentiating between lesions affecting different posterior cord structures. (基于特定感觉和运动功能障碍定位脊髓损伤，理解脊髓后部（背角、背索）的解剖组织及其与特定通路和核的关系，以及区分影响不同脊髓后部结构的损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a fall down a flight of stairs. He complains of severe neck pain and bilateral lower extremity weakness. On examination, his upper extremity strength is normal (5/5), but his lower extremity strength is significantly reduced (2/5 bilaterally). Sensory examination reveals complete loss of vibration and proprioception below the T10 dermatome bilaterally. Pain and temperature sensation are intact below the level of injury. Reflexes in the lower extremities are absent. MRI of the cervical spine reveals a complete transection of the spinal cord at the C6 level.

Which spinal cord structure is MOST likely responsible for the patient's loss of vibration and proprioception below T10?

(A) Lateral corticospinal tract
(B) Anterior spinocerebellar tract
(C) Fasciculus gracilis
(D) Fasciculus cuneatus
(E) Posterior spinocerebellar tract

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, fall, neck pain, bilateral lower extremity weakness. This suggests a spinal cord injury.
- **Motor Examination:** Normal upper extremity strength (5/5), reduced lower extremity strength (2/5 bilaterally). This indicates a lesion below the cervical enlargement (where upper limb innervation originates) but above the lumbar enlargement (where lower limb innervation originates). The complete transection at C6 explains the paraplegia.
- **Sensory Examination:** Complete loss of vibration and proprioception below T10 bilaterally. Pain and temperature sensation are intact. This is the crucial finding for localization. Vibration and proprioception are carried by the dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway. Pain and temperature are carried by the spinothalamic tract.
- **Reflexes:** Absent lower extremity reflexes are consistent with spinal shock or damage to the reflex arc below the level of injury.
- **MRI Findings:** Complete transection of the spinal cord at C6 confirms the level of injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific spinal cord structure responsible for the loss of vibration and proprioception.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- The dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway carries fine touch, vibration, and proprioception.
- This pathway ascends ipsilaterally in the spinal cord within the posterior funiculus.
- The posterior funiculus contains two main tracts: the fasciculus gracilis (medially) and the fasciculus cuneatus (laterally).
- The fasciculus gracilis carries sensory information from the lower limbs and lower trunk (below T6).
- The fasciculus cuneatus carries sensory information from the upper limbs and upper trunk (above T6).
- The lesion is at C6. Therefore, sensory information from the lower limbs and trunk (below T6) would travel in the fasciculus gracilis before decussating in the medulla. Sensory information from the upper limbs and trunk (above T6) would travel in the fasciculus cuneatus before decussating in the medulla.
- The patient has lost vibration and proprioception below T10. Since the lesion is at C6, the fasciculus gracilis (carrying information from below T6) is damaged. The fasciculus cuneatus (carrying information from above T6) is also damaged, but the question asks for the structure responsible for the *lower* extremity deficits.
- The fasciculus gracilis is the primary tract carrying vibration and proprioception from the lower limbs and lower trunk. Damage to this tract at C6 would cause loss of these sensations below the level of the lesion (T10 is mentioned, likely reflecting the level of sensory testing, but the lesion is at C6).
- The lateral corticospinal tract carries motor commands and is located in the lateral funiculus. Damage causes contralateral weakness below the lesion.
- The anterior spinocerebellar tract carries proprioceptive information from the lower limbs to the cerebellum and is located in the anterior funiculus. Damage affects coordination.
- The posterior spinocerebellar tract carries proprioceptive information from the upper limbs and trunk to the cerebellum and is located in the posterior funiculus, lateral to the dorsal columns. Damage affects coordination.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The spinal cord develops from the neural tube. The dorsal aspect of the neural tube gives rise to the posterior (dorsal) horn and the posterior funiculi (containing the dorsal columns). The dorsal columns (fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus) are derived from the dorsal part of the neural tube.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Lateral corticospinal tract: This tract is located in the lateral funiculus and carries motor commands for voluntary movement. Damage causes contralateral weakness and spasticity below the lesion level. This tract is responsible for the patient's lower extremity weakness, but not the loss of vibration and proprioception.
- (B) Anterior spinocerebellar tract: This tract is located in the anterior funiculus and carries proprioceptive information from the lower limbs to the cerebellum. Damage causes impaired coordination (ataxia) in the lower limbs, not loss of vibration and proprioception.
- (C) Fasciculus gracilis: This tract is part of the dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway, located in the medial posterior funiculus. It carries vibration and proprioception from the lower limbs and lower trunk (below T6). A lesion at C6 would damage this tract, causing the observed sensory deficits below T6 (and thus below T10). This is the correct answer.
- (E) Posterior spinocerebellar tract: This tract is located in the posterior funiculus, lateral to the dorsal columns. It carries proprioceptive information from the upper limbs and trunk to the cerebellum. Damage causes impaired coordination (ataxia) in the upper limbs and trunk, not loss of vibration and proprioception in the lower limbs.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure               | Location             | Function                                      | Lesion Effect (C6)                                                                 |
| :---------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Fasciculus Gracilis     | Medial Posterior Funiculus | Vibration, Proprioception (Lower Limb/Trunk) | Loss of vibration/proprioception below T6 (including lower limbs)                   |
| Fasciculus Cuneatus     | Lateral Posterior Funiculus | Vibration, Proprioception (Upper Limb/Trunk) | Loss of vibration/proprioception below T6 (including upper limbs)                   |
| Lateral Corticospinal Tract | Lateral Funiculus    | Motor (Voluntary Movement)                   | Contralateral weakness/paralysis below the lesion (lower limbs in this case)        |
| Anterior Spinocerebellar Tract | Anterior Funiculus   | Proprioception (Lower Limb -> Cerebellum)   | Ataxia/Coordination problems in lower limbs                                         |
| Posterior Spinocerebellar Tract | Posterior Funiculus   | Proprioception (Upper Limb/Trunk -> Cerebellum) | Ataxia/Coordination problems in upper limbs/trunk                                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway is responsible for carrying vibration and proprioception. Within the spinal cord, this pathway is divided into the fasciculus gracilis (lower body) and fasciculus cuneatus (upper body), located in the posterior funiculus. Lesions affecting these tracts cause loss of these specific sensory modalities below the level of the lesion. Differentiating between the fasciculus gracilis and cuneatus based on the body region affected is crucial for localizing spinal cord lesions. Remember that the spinothalamic tract carries pain and temperature, and the corticospinal tract carries motor function.

Key Differentials:
- Fasciculus cuneatus vs. gracilis: Differentiate based on the body region affected (upper vs. lower).
- Dorsal columns vs. Spinothalamic tract: Differentiate based on sensory modality (vibration/proprioception vs. pain/temperature).
- Dorsal columns vs. Corticospinal tract: Differentiate based on function (sensory vs. motor).
